, the challenger computes
\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m_{i})
and returns
\mathsf{ct}_{i}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
If
b=1
, the challenger chooses
\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and returns
\mathsf{ct}_{i}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

\mathpzc{A}
can repeat this process polynomially many times.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. This is the output of the experiment.

We say that
\Sigma
is ciphertext pseudorandom if, for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ct}\mbox{-}\mathsf{pr}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[ \expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ct}{cr}(\secp, 0)=1] - \Pr[ \expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ct}{pr}(\secp, 1)=1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(12)

Theorem 2.14.

If OWFs exist, there exists an SKE scheme that is ciphertext pseudorandom.

Public Key Encryption (PKE).

Definition 2.15 (Public Key Encryption (Syntax)).

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and let
p
,
q
and
r
be some polynomials.
A public key encryption scheme is a tuple of algorithms
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
with
plaintext space
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{n}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}
, public key space
\mathcal{PK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{q(\lambda)}

and secret key space
\mathcal{SK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{r(\lambda)}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
:

The key generation algorithm takes as input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}\in\mathcal{PK}
and a secret key
\mathsf{sk}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
:

The encryption algorithm takes as input
\mathsf{pk}
and a plaintext
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow m^{\prime}\mbox{ or }\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes as input
\mathsf{sk}
and
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs a plaintext
m^{\prime}
or
\bot
.

We require that a PKE scheme satisfies decryption correctness defined below.

Definition 2.16 (Decryption Correctness for PKE).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})\neq m\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

As security, we consider OW-CPA security or IND-CPA security defined below.

Definition 2.17 (OW-CPA Security for PKE).

Let
\ell
be a polynomial of the security parameter
\lambda
.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a PKE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

The challenger samples
(m^{1},\cdots,m^{\ell})\leftarrow\mathcal{M}^{\ell}
, computes
\mathsf{ct}^{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m^{i})
for all
i\in[\ell]
and sends
\{\mathsf{ct}^{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
m^{\prime}
.

4.

The output of the experiment is
1
if
m^{\prime}=m^{i}
for some
i\in[\ell]
. Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
0
.

We say that
\Sigma
is OW-CPA secure if, for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ow}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)=1]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(15)

Note that we assume
1/|\mathcal{M}|
is negligible.

Definition 2.18 (IND-CPA Security for PKE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a PKE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
, and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m_{b})
, and sends
\mathsf{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. This is the output of the experiment.

We say that
\Sigma
is IND-CPA secure if, for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ind}{cpa}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ind}{cpa}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(16)

It is well known that IND-CPA security implies OW-CPA security.
There are many IND-CPA secure PKE schemes against QPT adversaries under standard cryptographic assumptions.
A famous one is Regev PKE scheme, which is IND-CPA secure if the learning with errors (LWE) assumption holds against QPT adversaries
[
Reg09
]
.
See
[
Reg09
,
GPV08
]
for the LWE assumption and constructions of post-quantum PKE.

Encryption with Certified Deletion.

Broadbent and Islam
[
BI20
]
introduced the notion of encryption with certified deletion.

Definition 2.19 (One-Time SKE with Certified Deletion (Syntax)
[
BI20
,
HMNY21
]
).

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and let
p
,
q
and
r
be some polynomials.
A one-time secret key encryption scheme with certified deletion is a tuple of algorithms
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
with
plaintext space
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{n}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes p(\lambda)}
, key space
\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{q(\lambda)}
and deletion certificate space
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{r(\lambda)}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}
:

The key generation algorithm takes as input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}\in\mathcal{K}
.

\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)\rightarrow\mathpzc{ct}
:

The encryption algorithm takes as input
\mathsf{sk}
and a plaintext
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow m^{\prime}~or~\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes as input
\mathsf{sk}
and
\mathpzc{ct}
, and outputs a plaintext
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
or
\bot
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm takes as input
\mathpzc{ct}
, and outputs a certification
\mathsf{cert}\in\mathcal{D}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top
or
\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}
and
\mathsf{cert}
as input, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

We require that a one-time SKE scheme with certified deletion satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition 2.20 (Correctness for One-Time SKE with Certified Deletion).

There are two types of correctness, namely,
decryption correctness and verification correctness.

Decryption Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[m^{\prime}\neq m\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{ct}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{ct}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

We additionally require verification correctness with QOTP in this work.
This is because we need it for the construction of FE in
Section

4.3
.
This notion means that even if we encrypt a ciphertext with quantum one-time pad (QOTP), we can run the original deletion algorithm
\mathpzc{Del}
and recover a valid certificate by using the QOTP key.
In fact, the construction of
[
BI20
]
satisfies verification correctness with QOTP as well.

Definition 2.21 (Verification Correctness with QOTP).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
and a PPT algorithm
\mathsf{Recover}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{cert}^{*})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathpzc{ct}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}\\
\widetilde{\mathsf{cert}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(Z^{b}X^{a}\mathpzc{ct}X^{a}Z^{b})\\
\mathsf{cert}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Recover}(a,b,\widetilde{\mathsf{cert}})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

We require that a one-time SKE with certified deletion satisfies certified deletion security defined below.

Definition 2.22 (Certified Deletion Security for One-Time SKE with Certified Deletion).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a one-time SKE scheme with certified deletion.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{otsk}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{del}}(\lambda,b)
against an unbounded adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger computes
\mathpzc{ct}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m_{b})
and sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger.

5.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{cert})
. If the output is
\bot
, the challenger sends
\bot
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
If the output is
\top
, the challenger sends
\mathsf{sk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. This is the output of the experiment.

We say that
\Sigma
is OT-CD secure if, for any unbounded
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{otsk}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{del}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[ \expc{\Sigma,\qA}{otsk}{cert}{del}(\secp, 0)=1] - \Pr[ \expc{\Sigma,\qA}{otsk}{cert}{del}(\secp, 1)=1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(28)

Theorem 2.23 (
[
BI20
]
).

There exists one-time SKE with certified deletion that satisfies
Definitions

2.19
,
2.20
,
2.22
and
2.21
exists unconditionally.

Attribute-Based Encryption.

Definition 2.24 (KP-ABE (Syntax)).

A key-policy ABE (KP-ABE) scheme is a tuple of PPT algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\allowbreak\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
with a class of predicates
\mathcal{P}_{n}
(represented as class of boolean circuits with
n
input bits), and a message space
\mathcal{M}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

The setup algorithm takes as input a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},P)
:

The key generation algorithm takes as input the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and a predicate
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{P}
corresponding to the predicate
P
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},a,m)
:

The encryption algorithm takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
, an attribute
a\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{P},\mathsf{ct})
:

The decryption algorithm takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{P}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs a classical message
m^{\prime}
or
\bot
.

Definition 2.25 (Correctness of KP-ABE).

The correctness of KP-ABE for a class of predicates
\mathcal{P}_{n}
and a message space
\mathcal{M}
is defined as follows.

Decryption correctness:

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N},P\in\mathcal{P}_{n},a\in\{0,1\}^{n},m\in\mathcal{M}
such that
P(a)=1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{P},\mathpzc{ct})\neq m\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{P}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},P)\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},a,m)\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition 2.26 (IND-CPA Security of KP-ABE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a KP-ABE scheme for a class predicates
\mathcal{P}_{n}
and message space
\mathcal{M}
. We consider the following experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
.

1.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends a predicate
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, called key query, to the challenger and it returns
\mathsf{sk}_{P_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},P)
.
\mathpzc{A}
can send unbounded polynomially many key queries. Let
q
be the total number of key queries.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends a challenge message pair
(m_{0},m_{1})
and an attribute
a\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\absolutevalue{m_0}=\absolutevalue{m_1}
.

4.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{ct}_{b}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},a,m_{b})
. It sends
\mathsf{ct}_{b}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
can send key queries again.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs its guess
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. If
P_{i}(a)=0
for all
i\in[q]
, the experiments outputs
b^{\prime}
.

We say that the
\Sigma
is adaptively secure if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(32)

We can define similar experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
where
\mathpzc{A}
is restricted to submit the challenge attribute
a\in\{0,1\}^{n}
before it receives
\mathsf{pk}
from the challenger. We say that the
\Sigma
is selectively secure if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(33)

Theorem 2.27 (
[
GVW15a
,
BGG+14
]
).

If the LWE assumption holds, there exists selectively secure KP-ABE for all boolean circuits. In addition, if the LWE assumption holds against sub-exponential time algorithms, there exists adaptively secure KP-ABE for all boolean circuits.

Classical Fully Homomorphic Encryption.

Definition 2.28 (Leveled Fully Homomorphic Encryption).

A leveled FHE is a tuple of PPT algorithms
(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\allowbreak\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Dec})
with a class of circuits
\mathcal{C}=\{\mathcal{C}_{d}\}_{d\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
\mathcal{C}_{d}
contains all Boolean circuits of depth up to
d
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda},1^{d})
:

The key generation algorithm takes as input the security parameters
1^{\lambda}
and
1^{d}
and outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x)
:

The encryption algorithm takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a message
x\in\{0,1\}
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},C,\mathsf{ct}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{ct}_{n})
:

The evaluation algorithm takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
, a circuit
C\in\mathcal{C}
, ciphertexts
\mathsf{ct}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{ct}_{n}
where
n
denotes the input length of the circuit
C
, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{C}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct})
:

The decryption algorithm takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
, and outputs a message
x^{\prime}
or
\bot
.

Definition 2.29 (Compactness).

A classical FHE is compact if there exists a fixed polynomial bound
B(\cdot)
so that, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, any
C\in\mathcal{C}
, and plaintext
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\absolutevalue{\ct_C}\leq B(\lambda)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x_{i})\\
\mathsf{ct}_{C}\leftarrow\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},C,\mathsf{ct}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{ct}_{n})\end{array}\right]=1.

Definition 2.30 (Correctness).

An FHE scheme is said to be correct for
\mathcal{C}
if for any
\lambda,n\in\mathbb{N},C\in\mathcal{C},\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{ct}_{C})\neq C(\bm{x})\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x_{i})\\
\mathsf{ct}_{C}\leftarrow\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},C,\mathsf{ct}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{ct}_{n})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition 2.31 (Security of FHE).

An FHE scheme
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
with a class of circuits
\mathcal{C}
is said to be IND-CPA secure if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, any
\lambda,n\in\mathbb{N}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{A}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{ct})=1\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda}),\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},0)\end{array}\right]-\Pr\left[\mathpzc{A}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{ct})=1\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda}),\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},1)\end{array}\right]={\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Theorem 2.32 (
[
BV14
,
GSW13
]
).

If the LWE assumption holds, there exists leveled FHE.

Indistinguishability Obfuscation

Definition 2.33 (Indistinguishability Obfuscator
[
BGI+12
]
).

A PPT algorithm
i\mathcal{O}
is a secure IO for a classical circuit class
\{\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
if it satisfies the following two conditions.

Functionality:

For any security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, circuit
C\in\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}
, and input
x
, we have that

\displaystyle\Pr[C^{\prime}(x)=C(x)\mid C^{\prime}\leftarrow i\mathcal{O}(C)]=1\kern 5.0pt.

(44)

Indistinguishability:

For any PPT
\mathsf{Sampler}
and QPT distinguisher
\mathpzc{D}
, the following holds:

If
\Pr[\forall x\ C_{0}(x)=C_{1}(x)\land\absolutevalue{C_0}=\absolutevalue{C_1}\mid(C_{0},C_{1},\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sampler}(1^{\lambda})]>1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{i\mathcal{O},\mathpzc{D}}^{\mathsf{io}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left|\Pr\left[\mathpzc{D}(i\mathcal{O}(C_{0}),\mathsf{aux})=1\mid(C_{0},C_{1},\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sampler}(1^{\lambda})\right]\right.

(45)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~\left.-\Pr\left[\mathpzc{D}(i\mathcal{O}(C_{1}),\mathsf{aux})=1\mid(C_{0},C_{1},\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sampler}(1^{\lambda})\right]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(46)

There are a few candidates of secure IO for polynomial-size classical circuits against quantum adversaries
[
BGMZ18
,
CHVW19
,
AP20
]
.

Obfuscation for compute-and-compare programs.

Definition 2.34 (Compute-and-Compare Circuits).

A compute-and-compare circuit
\mathbf{CC}[P,\mathsf{lock},m]
is of the form

\mathbf{CC}[P,\mathsf{lock},m](x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}m&(P(x)=\mathsf{lock})\\
0&(\text{otherwise})~,\end{array}\right.

(47)

where
P
is a circuit,
\mathsf{lock}
is a string called lock value, and
m
is a message.

We assume that a program
P
has an associated set of parameters
\mathsf{pp}_{P}
(input size, output size, circuit size) which we do not need to hide.

Definition 2.35 (Compute-and-Compare Obfuscation).

A PPT algorithm
\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}
is a secure obfuscator for the family of distributions
D=\{D_{\mathsf{param}}\}_{\mathsf{param}}
if the following holds:

Functionality Preserving:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for all program
P
, all lock value
\mathsf{lock}
, and all message
m
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr[\forall x,\widetilde{P}(x)=\mathbf{CC}[P,\mathsf{lock},m](x)\mid\widetilde{P}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},P,\mathsf{lock},m)]\geq 1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(48)

Distributional Indistinguishability:

There exists an efficient simulator
\mathsf{Sim}
such that for all
\mathpzc{D}
,
\mathsf{param}
and message
m
, we have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Pr[\qD(\CCObf(1^\secp,P,\lock,m),\qaux)=1] -\Pr[\qD(\Sim(1^\secp,\pp_P,1^\abs{m}),\qaux)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda),

(49)

where
(P,\mathsf{lock},\mathpzc{aux})\leftarrow D_{\mathsf{param}}
.

Theorem 2.36 (
[
GKW17
,
WZ17
]
).

If the LWE assumption holds, there exists compute-and-compare obfuscation for all families of distributions
D=\{D_{\mathsf{param}}\}
, where each
D_{\mathsf{param}}
outputs uniformly random lock value
\mathsf{lock}
independent of
P
and
\mathpzc{aux}
.

3
Collusion-Resistant Functional Encryption with Certified Everlasting Deletion

In this section, we present the definitions of FE with certified everlasting deletion and a collusion-resistant construction.

3.1
Definitions

First, we introduce the syntax and security definitions of FE with certified everlasting deletion.

Definition 3.1 (Functional Encryption with Certified Everlasting Deletion).

A functional encryption with certified everlasting deletion for a class
\mathcal{F}
of functions is a tuple of QPT algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}
, master public key space
\mathcal{MPK}
, master secret key space
\mathcal{MSK}
, and secret key space
\mathcal{SK}
,
that works as follows.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})
:

The setup algorithm takes the security parameter as input, and outputs a master public key
\mathsf{MPK}\in\mathcal{MPK}
and a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}\in\mathcal{MSK}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)
:

The key generation algorithm takes
\mathsf{MSK}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
as input, and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\rightarrow(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk})
:

The encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{MPK}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
as input, and outputs a ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}\in\mathcal{C}
and a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow y
or
\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and
\mathpzc{ct}
as input, and outputs
y
or
\bot
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm takes the ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}
as input, and outputs a classical certificate
\mathsf{cert}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top
or
\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes
\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathsf{cert}
as input, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Definition 3.2 (Correctness of Functional Encryption with Certified Everlasting Deletion).

The correctness of FE with certified everlasting deletion for a class of functions
\mathcal{F}
and plaintext space
\mathcal{M}
is defined as follows.

Evaluation Correctness:

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[y\neq f(m)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\\
(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
y\leftarrow\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification Correctness:

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\neq\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Remark
3.3.

In FE, we should be able to run
\mathpzc{Dec}
algorithm for many different functions
f
on the same ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.
One might think that the quantum
\mathpzc{ct}
is destroyed by
\mathpzc{Dec}
algorithm, and it can be used only once.
However, it is easy to see that
\mathpzc{Dec}
algorithm can be always modified so that it does not disturb the quantum state
\mathpzc{ct}

by using the gentle measurement lemma
[
Win99
]
thanks to the evaluation correctness.

Security notions.

We define an indistinguishability-based security notion in the collusion-resistant setting in this section.
We extend the certified everlasting security notion of PKE by Bartusek and Khurana
[
BK23
]
to the FE setting to obtain our indistinguishability-based security notion.

Definition 3.4 (Certified Everlasting Indistinguishable-Security of FE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a functional encryption with certified everlasting deletion for a class
\mathcal{F}
of functions, plaintext space
\mathcal{M}
. We consider two experiments
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
and
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
played between a challenger and a non-uniform QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}=\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda},\ket{\psi}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
. The experiments are defined as follows:

1.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}(\ket{\psi}_{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
is allowed to make arbitrarily many key queries.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger computes
(\mathpzc{ct}_{b},\mathsf{vk}_{b})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m_{b})
, and sends
\mathsf{ct}_{b}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
is allowed to make arbitrarily many key queries.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
.

6.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
sends a certificate of deletion
\mathsf{cert}
and its internal state
\rho
to the challenger.

7.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{b},\mathsf{cert})
. If the outcome is
\top
and
f_{\ell}(m_{0})=f_{\ell}(m_{1})
holds for all key queries
f_{\ell}
, the experiment
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
outputs
\rho
; otherwise
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
outputs
\bot
.

8.

The challenger sends the outcome of
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{b},\mathsf{cert})
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
.

9.

Again,
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
is allowed to make arbitrarily many key queries. For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
.

10.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
outputs its guess
b^{\prime}
. If
f_{\ell}(m_{0})=f_{\ell}(m_{1})
holds for all key queries
f_{\ell}
, the experiment
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
outputs
b^{\prime}
; otherwise
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
outputs
\bot
.

We say that the
\Sigma
is adaptively certified everlasting indistinguishable-secure if for any non-uniform QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}=\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda},\ket{\psi}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0),\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda),

(57)

and

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(58)

3.2
Tools

We introduce a few tools for FE with certified everlasting deletion in this section.

(Interactive) certified everlasting lemma.

First, we recall the certified everlasting lemma by Bartusek and Khurana
[
BK23
]
.

Lemma 3.5 (Certified Everlasting Lemma
[
BK23
, ePrint Ver. 20221122:050839]
).

Let
\{\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\theta)\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}}
be a family of distributions over either classical bit strings or quantum states, and let
\mathfrak{A}
be any class of adversaries such that for any
\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{A}
, it holds that

\bigg|\Pr[\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\theta))=1]-\Pr[\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(0^{\lambda}))=1]\bigg|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(59)

For any
\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{A}
, consider the following distribution
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lambda}^{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}}(b)\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N},b\in\{0,1\}}
over quantum states, obtained by running
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
as follows:

•

Sample
z,\theta\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and initialize
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
with
(\ket{z}_{\theta},b\oplus\bigoplus_{i:\theta_{i}=0}z_{i},\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\theta))
.

•

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
’s output is parsed as bit string
z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and a residual quantum state
\rho
.

•

If
z_{i}=z_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
such that
\theta_{i}=1
then output
\rho
, and otherwise output a special symbol
\bot
.

Then, it holds that

\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lambda}^{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}}(0),\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lambda}^{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}}(1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(60)

Remark
3.6.

Although the description of the lemma above is slightly different from the original version (see the first item of
Remark

3.8
), it is essentially the same as what Bartusek and Khurana
[
BK23
]
proved. In addition, even if we put
b\oplus\bigoplus_{i:\theta_{i}=0}z_{i}
in
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}
with
\theta
, the lemma holds.

We can generalize
Lemma

3.5
to a variant in the interactive game setting as follows.

Lemma 3.7 (Interactive Certified Everlasting Lemma
[
BK23
]
).

For interactive QPT algorithms
\mathpzc{A}
and
\mathpzc{C}
,
\theta\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, and
\beta\in\{0,1\}
, let
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)

be an experiment that works as follows:

•

\mathpzc{A}
takes
1^{\lambda}
as input and
\mathpzc{C}
takes
(1^{\lambda},\theta,\beta)
as input.

•

\mathpzc{A}
and
\mathpzc{C}
interact with each other through a quantum channel.

•

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
, which is treated as the output of the experiment.

For interactive QPT algorithms
\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathpzc{C}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{A}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{}}(\lambda,b)

be an experiment that works as follows:

•

Sample
z,\theta\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

•

\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
takes
(1^{\lambda},\ket{z}_{\theta})
as input and
\mathpzc{C}
takes
(1^{\lambda},\theta,b\oplus\bigoplus_{i:\theta_{i}=0}z_{i})
as input.

•

\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathpzc{C}
interact with each other through a quantum channel.

•

\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
outputs a string
z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and a quantum state
\rho
.

•

If
z_{i}=z_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
such that
\theta_{i}=1
then the experiment outputs
\rho
, and otherwise it outputs a special symbol
\bot
.

For a QPT algorithm
\mathpzc{C}
, if for any QPT algorithm
\mathpzc{A}
,
\theta\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, and
\beta\in\{0,1\}
, it holds that

\bigg|\Pr[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{}}(\lambda,0^{\lambda},\beta)=1]\bigg|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda),

(61)

then for any QPT algorithm
\mathpzc{A}^{\prime}
, it holds that

\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{A}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{}}(\lambda,0),\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{A}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(62)

Remark
3.8.

There are the following three differences from the certified everlasting lemma of
[
BK23
]
besides notational adaptations.

1.

The challenger can use
\theta
in an arbitrary manner whereas they require the challenger to use
\theta
in a bit-by-bit manner.
17
17

17

In their notation, the challenger corresponds to
\mathcal{Z}(\theta)
.

2.

We consider an interactive setting whereas they consider a non-interactive setting.

3.

The challenger also takes
b\oplus\bigoplus_{i:\theta_{i}=0}z_{i}
as part of its input.

Indeed, we believe that the above variant is implicitly used in the security proof of their certified everlasting secure ABE in
[
BK23
]
.
We observe that the above variant can be proven in essentially the same way as their original proof.

Public-Slot functional encryption.

We introduce a new primitive which we call public-slot functional encryption. In this primitive, a decryption key is associated with two-input function where the first and second inputs are referred to as secret and public inputs, respectively. Given a ciphertext of a message
m
and a decryption key for a two-input function
f
, one can compute
f(m,\mathsf{pub})
for any public input
\mathsf{pub}
. In the security experiment, we require that a pair of challenge messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
must satisfy
f(m_{0},\mathsf{pub})=f(m_{1},\mathsf{pub})
for all key queries
f
and public inputs
\mathsf{pub}
to prevent trivial attacks.
A formal definition is given below.

Definition 3.9 (Public-Slot FE (Syntax)).

A public-slot functional encryption scheme for a class
\mathcal{F}
of functions
is a tuple of PPT algorithms

\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})

with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}
, master public key space
\mathcal{MPK}
, master secret key space
\mathcal{MSK}
, secret key space
\mathcal{SK}
, and public input space
\mathcal{P}
,
that work as follows.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})
:

The setup algorithm takes the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input, and outputs a master public key
\mathsf{MPK}\in\mathcal{MPK}

and a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}\in\mathcal{MSK}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}_{f}
:

The key generation algorithm takes
\mathsf{MSK}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
as input,
and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\rightarrow\mathsf{CT}
:

The encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{MPK}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
as input,
and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT},\mathsf{pub})\rightarrow y

\mbox{\bf or }\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
,
\mathsf{CT}
, and a public input
\mathsf{pub}\in\mathcal{P}
as input, and outputs
y
or
\bot
.

We require that an FE with certified everlasting deletion scheme satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition 3.10 (Correctness of Public-Slot FE).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
,
f\in\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathsf{pub}\in\mathcal{P}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[y\neq f(m,\mathsf{pub})\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\\
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
y\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT},\mathsf{pub})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition 3.11 (Security of Public-Slot FE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a public-slot FE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to make arbitrarily many key queries.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m_{b})
and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

Again,
\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to make arbitrarily many key queries.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.
If
f_{\ell}(m_{0},\mathsf{pub})=f_{\ell}(m_{1},\mathsf{pub})
holds for all key queries
f_{\ell}
and public inputs
\mathsf{pub}\in\mathcal{P}
, the experiment outputs
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is adaptively indistinguishable-secure if for any
QPT
adversary
\mathpzc{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ada}{ind}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ada}{ind}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(67)

It is easy to construct selectively single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE from multi-input FE by Goldwasser et al., which can be instantiated with IO
[
GGG+14
]
.
We convert it to adaptively indistinguishable-secure public-slot PKFE via a few transformation.
We prove the following theorem in
Section

B.4
.

Theorem 3.12.

If there exist IO and OWFs, there exists an adaptively indistinguishable-secure public-slot PKFE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
.

3.3
Collusion-Resistant Construction

Ingredients.

We need the following building blocks.

•

Public-slot FE
\mathsf{FE}=\mathsf{FE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
for all polynomial-size circuits.

•

PRG
\mathsf{PRG}:\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{2\lambda}
.

Scheme description.

Our FE with certified everlasting deletion scheme
\mathsf{CED}=\mathsf{CED}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
is described below.

\mathsf{CED}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Output
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK}
.

\mathsf{CED}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},f)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK}
.

2.

Generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f])
where
g[f]
is a function described in
Figure

1
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}
.

\mathsf{CED}.\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{MPK}=\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK}
.

2.

Generate
z_{i},\theta_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for every
i\in[2n+1]
.

3.

Generate
u_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and compute
v_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j,b})
for every
i\in[2n+1]
,
j\in[\lambda]
, and
b\in\{0,1\}
.
Set
U\coloneqq(u_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and
V\coloneqq(v_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

4.

Generate a state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,j}}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\ket{z_{i,j}}\ket{u_{i,j,z_{i,j}}}&\text{~if~}\theta_{i,j}=0\\
\ket{0}\ket{u_{i,j,0}}+(-1)^{z_{i,j}}\ket{1}\ket{u_{i,j,1}}&\text{~if~}\theta_{i,j}=1\end{cases}

(68)

where
\theta_{i,j}
(resp.
z_{i,j}
) is the
j
-th bit of
\theta_{i}
(resp.
z_{i}
)
for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
.

5.

Generate

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n]\\
0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n+1,2n+1]\end{cases}.

(69)

6.

Generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},V\|\theta_{1}\|\ldots\|\theta_{2n+1}\|\beta_{1}\|\ldots\|\beta_{2n+1})
.

7.

Output
\mathpzc{ct}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}})
and
\mathsf{vk}=(U,(z_{i},\theta_{i})_{i\in[2n+1]})
.

\mathsf{CED}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathpzc{ct})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}
and
\mathpzc{ct}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}})
.

2.

Coherently apply
\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\cdot)
on
\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}}

and measure the outcome
y
.

3.

Output
y
.

\mathsf{CED}.\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})
:

1.

Parse
\mathpzc{ct}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}})
.

2.

Measure
\ket{\psi_{i,j}}
in the Hadamard basis to get
c_{i,j}\|d_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}
for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i,j},d_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}
.

\mathsf{CED}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=(U,(z_{i},\theta_{i})_{i\in[2n+1]})
and
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i,j},d_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}
.

2.

Check if
z_{i,j}=c_{i,j}\oplus d_{i,j}\cdot(u_{i,j,0}\oplus u_{i,j,1})
holds for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=1
, where
z_{i,j}
is the
j
-th bit of
z_{i}
. If so, output
\top
and otherwise output
\bot
.

g[f]

Secret Input:

V,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2n+1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{2n+1}

Public Input:

(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}

1.

Parse
V=(v_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

2.

Check if
\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j})=v_{i,j,b_{i,j}}
holds for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
. If so, go to the next step and otherwise output
\bot
.

3.

Compute
m_{i}\coloneqq\beta_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}b_{i,j}
for every
i\in[2n+1]
.

4.

Output
f(m_{1}\|\cdots\|m_{n})
if
m_{2n+1}=0
and output
f(m_{n+1}\|\cdots\|m_{2n})
otherwise.

Figure 1:
The description of the function
g[f]

Theorem 3.13.

If
\mathsf{FE}
is adaptively indistinguishable-secure public-slot FE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
and
\mathsf{PRG}
is a secure PRG,
\mathsf{CED}
is adaptively certified everlasting indistinguishable-secure FE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
.

Decryption Correctness.

Let
\mathpzc{ct}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}})
be an honestly generated ciphertext for a message
m
and
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}
be an honestly generated decryption key for a function
f
. Then we have

\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}\in\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},V\|\theta_{1}\|\ldots\|\theta_{2n+1}\|\beta_{1}\|\ldots\|\beta_{2n+1})
and
\ket{\psi_{i,j}}=\ket{z_{i,j}}\ket{u_{i,j,z_{i,j}}}
for all
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
. Since we have
\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j,b})=v_{i,j,b}
for all
i\in[2n+1]
,
j\in[\lambda]
, and
b\in\{0,1\}
, and
m_{2n+1}=\beta_{2n+1}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{2n+1,j}=0}z_{i,j}=0
, if we coherently run
g[f](V\|\theta_{1}\|\ldots\|\theta_{2n+1}\|\beta_{1}\|\ldots\|\beta_{2n+1},\cdot)
on
\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}}

and measure the output, then the resulting outcome is
f(\beta_{1}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{1,j}=0}z_{1,j}\|\ldots\|\beta_{n}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{n,j}=0}z_{n,j})=f(m)
.
Then the decryption correctness follows from that of
\mathsf{FE}
.

Verification Correctness.

Let
\mathpzc{ct}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}})
be an honestly generated ciphertext and
\mathsf{vk}=(U,(z_{i},\theta_{i})_{i\in[2n+1]})
be the corresponding verification key.
For all
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=1
,
since we have
\ket{\psi_{i,j}}=\ket{0}\ket{u_{i,j,0}}+(-1)^{z_{i,j}}\ket{1}\ket{u_{i,j,1}}
, if we measure it in the Hadamard basis, then the outcome
c_{i}\|d_{i}
satisfies
z_{i,j}=c_{i,j}\oplus d_{i,j}\cdot(u_{i,j,0}\oplus u_{i,j,1})
.
This immediately implies the verification correctness.

Security for
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{CED},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
.

We omit the proof in the main body. See
Appendix

A
.

Security for
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{CED},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
.

Let
\mathpzc{A}
be an adversary against the adaptive certified everlasting indistinguishable-security.
We consider the following sequence of hybrids.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the original everlasting adaptive security experiment where the challenge bit is set to be
0
. Specifically, it works as follows:

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK}
, and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
can make arbitrarily many key queries at any point of the experiment. When it makes a key query
f
, the challenger generates
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f])
and returns
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m^{(0)},m^{(1)})
to the challenger.
18
18

18

We use
(m^{(0)},m^{(1)})
instead of
(m_{0},m_{1})
to denote a pair of challenge messages to avoid a notational collision.
It must satisfy
f(m^{(0)})=f(m^{(1)})
for all key queries
f
that are made before or after sending
(m^{(0)},m^{(1)})
.

4.

The challenger generates
(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m^{(0)})
. Specifically,

(a)

Generate
z_{i},\theta_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for every
i\in[2n+1]
.

(b)

Generate
u_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and compute
v_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j,b})
for every
i\in[2n+1]
,
j\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
and set
U=(u_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and
V\coloneqq(v_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

(c)

Generate a state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,j}}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\ket{z_{i,j}}\ket{u_{i,j,z_{i,j}}}&\text{~if~}\theta_{i,j}=0\\
\ket{0}\ket{u_{i,j,0}}+(-1)^{z_{i,j}}\ket{1}\ket{u_{i,j,1}}&\text{~if~}\theta_{i,j}=1\end{cases}

(70)

where
\theta_{i,j}
(resp.
z_{i,j}
) is the
j
-th bit of
\theta_{i}
(resp.
z_{i}
)
for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
.

(d)

Generate

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m_{i}^{(0)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n]\\
0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n+1,2n+1]\end{cases}.

(71)

(e)

Generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},V\|\theta_{1}\|\ldots\|\theta_{2n+1}\|\beta_{1}\|\ldots\|\beta_{2n+1})
.

(f)

Set
\mathpzc{ct}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}})
and
\mathsf{vk}=(U,(z_{i},\theta_{i})_{i\in[2n+1]})
.

The challenger sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i,j},d_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}
and its internal state
\rho
to the challenger.

6.

The challenger checks if
z_{i,j}=c_{i,j}\oplus d_{i,j}\cdot(u_{i,j,0}\oplus u_{i,j,1})
holds for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=1
. If it does not hold, the challenger outputs
\bot
as a final output of the experiment. Otherwise, go to the next step.

7.

The experiment outputs
\rho
as a final output.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
:

For
k=0,1,\ldots,n
, this hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except that
the way of generating
\beta_{i}
is modified as follows:

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m_{i}^{(0)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n]\\

Remark that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,0}
is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
.

This hybrid is identical to

except that

:

, this hybrid is identical to

Proof.

For applying
Lemma

3.7
, we consider the following experiment
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
between a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
for
\theta\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and
\beta\in\{0,1\}
as follows:

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
:

In this experiment,
\mathpzc{B}
and
\mathpzc{C}
play the roles of
\mathpzc{A}
and the challenger of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
with the differences that

and

,
\mathpzc{C}
does not generate
\ket{\psi_{\msglen+k,j}}
for

and thus not send it to
\mathpzc{B}
,
\mathpzc{C}
additionally sends
\{u_{n+k,j,b}\}_{j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
to
\mathpzc{B}
,
and
\mathpzc{B}
finally outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
instead of a certificate. Specifically, it works as follows:

1.

\mathpzc{C}
generates
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK}
, and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}
can make arbitrarily many key queries at any point of the experiment. When it makes a key query
f
,
\mathpzc{C}
generates
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f])
and returns
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

3.

\mathpzc{B}
sends
(m^{(0)},m^{(1)})
to
\mathpzc{C}
. It must satisfy
f(m^{(0)})=f(m^{(1)})
for all key queries
f
that are made before or after sending
(m^{(0)},m^{(1)})
.

4.

\mathpzc{C}
does the following:

(a)

Generate
z_{i},\theta_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for every
i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\}
and set
\theta_{n+k}\coloneqq\theta
.

(b)

Generate
u_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and compute
v_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j,b})
for every
i\in[2n+1]
,
j\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
and set
U=(u_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and
V\coloneqq(v_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

(c)

Generate a state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,j}}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\ket{z_{i,j}}\ket{u_{i,j,z_{i,j}}}&\text{~if~}\theta_{i,j}=0\\
\ket{0}\ket{u_{i,j,0}}+(-1)^{z_{i,j}}\ket{1}\ket{u_{i,j,1}}&\text{~if~}\theta_{i,j}=1\end{cases}

(83)

where
\theta_{i,j}
(resp.
z_{i,j}
) is the
j
-th bit of
\theta_{i}
(resp.
z_{i}
)
for every
i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\}
and
j\in[\lambda]
.

(d)

Generate

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m_{i}^{(0)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n]\\
m_{i-n}^{(1)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n+1,n+k-1]\\
\beta&\text{if~}i=n+k\\
0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n+k+1,2n+1]\end{cases}.

(84)

(e)

Generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},V\|\theta_{1}\|\ldots\|\theta_{2n+1}\|\beta_{1}\|\ldots\|\beta_{2n+1})
.

\mathpzc{C}
sends
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\},j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}},\{u_{n+k,j,b}\}_{j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}})
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

5.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
as a final output of the experiment.

We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.19.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{B}
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)=1]-\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0^{n},\beta)=1]\bigg|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(85)

Before proving
Lemma

3.19
, we complete the proof of
Lemma

3.14
assuming that
Lemma

3.19
is true.
By
Lemmas

3.7
and
3.19
, for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0),\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(86)

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,b)
is an experiment that works as follows:

\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1.k}}(\lambda,b)
:

1.

Sample
z,\theta\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
takes
(1^{\lambda},\ket{z}_{\theta})
as input.

3.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
interacts with
\mathpzc{C}
as in
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\theta,b\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j})
where
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
plays the role of
\mathpzc{B}
.

4.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
outputs a string
z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and a quantum state
\rho
.

5.

If
z_{j}=z_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{j}=1
then the experiment outputs
\rho
, and otherwise it outputs a special symbol
\bot
.

Note that the only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
is that
\beta_{n+k}
is set to be
0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
m_{k}^{(1)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
. If
m_{k}^{(1)}=0
, then there is no difference. Thus, we assume that
m_{k}^{(1)}=1
.
Then we construct
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
that distinguishes
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1.k}}(\lambda,0)
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1.k}}(\lambda,1)
using
\mathpzc{A}
that distinguishes
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
as follows.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda},\ket{z}_{\theta})
:

1.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
plays the role of
\mathpzc{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
where the external challenger
\mathpzc{C}
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,b)
is used to simulate the challenger of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
.
\mathpzc{C}
provides everything that should be sent to
\mathpzc{A}
except for
\ket{\psi_{\msglen+k,j}}
for
j\in[\lambda]
.
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
generates
\ket{\psi_{\msglen+k,j}}
by applying the map
\ket{b}\rightarrow\ket{b}\ket{u_{\msglen+k,j,b}}
on the
j
-th qubit of
\ket{z}_{\theta}
and uses it as part of
\mathpzc{ct}
sent to
\mathpzc{A}
. Note that this is possible since
(u_{n+k,j,b})_{j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
is provided from
\mathpzc{C}
.

2.

Suppose that
\mathpzc{A}
returns a certificate
(c_{i,j},d_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}
.
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
sets
z^{\prime}_{n+k,j}=c_{n+k,j}\oplus d_{n+k,j}\cdot(u_{n+k,j,0}\oplus u_{n+k,j,1})
for
j\in[\lambda]
. Again, note that this is possible since
(u_{n+k,j,b})_{j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
is provided from
\mathpzc{C}
.

3.

Output
z^{\prime}\coloneqq z^{\prime}_{n+k,1}\|\ldots\|z^{\prime}_{n+k,\lambda}
and
\mathpzc{A}
’s internal state
\rho
.

We can see that
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
if
b=0
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
if
b=1
. (Recall that we are assuming
m_{k}^{(1)}=1
.)
Moreover, we have
z_{j}=z_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\in[\lambda]
(which is the condition to not output
\bot
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1.k}}(\lambda,b)
) whenever
z_{i,j}=c_{i,j}\oplus d_{i,j}\cdot(u_{i,j,0}\oplus u_{i,j,1})
holds for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=1
(which is the condition to not output
\bot
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
).
Therefore, we must have

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k})\leq\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0),\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,1)).

(87)

Combined with
Equation

86
, this completes the proof of
Lemma

3.14
.
∎

Now, we are left to prove
Lemma

3.19
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.19
.

We further consider the following sequence of hybrids:

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
except that
v_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
is uniformly chosen from
\{0,1\}^{2\lambda}
instead of being set to be
\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}})
for all
i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\}
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
.

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,b}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
:

This is identical to
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
except that
\theta_{n+k}=\theta
is replaced with
0^{n}
. Note that
\theta_{n+k}
only appears in the encrypted message for
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}
in
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
.

Proposition 3.20.

If
\mathsf{PRG}
is a secure PRG,

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)=1]-\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)=1]\bigg|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(88)

Proof.

Noting that
u_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
for
i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\}
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
is used only for generating
v_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
in
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
,
Proposition

3.20
directly follows from the security of
\mathsf{PRG}
.
∎

Proposition 3.21.

If

\mathsf{FE}
is adaptively secure,

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)=1]-\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,b}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)=1]\bigg|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(89)

Proof.

For each
i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\}
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
, there is no
u
such that
\mathsf{PRG}(u)=v_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
except for probability
2^{-\lambda}
. Let
\mathsf{Good}
be the event that the above holds for all
i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\}
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
. We have
\Pr[\mathsf{Good}]\geq 1-2n\lambda 2^{-\lambda}=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
We prove that whenever
\mathsf{Good}
occurs, we have

\displaystyle g[f]((V,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2n+1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{2n+1}),(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]})

(90)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g[f]((V,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n+k-1},0^{n},\theta_{n+k+1,\ldots}\theta_{2n+1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{2n+1}),(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]})

(91)

for all key queries
f
and
(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}
. If this is proven,
Proposition

3.21
directly follows from the adaptive security of
\mathsf{FE}
.

Below, we prove
Equation

90
.
We consider the following two cases.

•

If
\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j})=v_{i,j,b_{i,j}}
holds for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
, then by the assumption that
\mathsf{Good}
occurs, we have
b_{i,j}=z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\}
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
. Then we have
\beta_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}b_{i,j}=m^{(0)}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
and
\beta_{2n+1}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{2n+1,j}=0}b_{2n+1,j}=0
. Then both sides of
Equation

90
are equal to
f(m^{(0)})
.

•

Otherwise, both sides of
Equation

90
are equal to
\bot
.

In either case,
Equation

90
holds. This completes the proof of
Proposition

3.21
.
∎

Proposition 3.22.

If
\mathsf{PRG}
is a secure PRG,

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,b}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)=1]-\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0^{n},\beta)=1]\bigg|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(92)

Proof.

Noting that
u_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
for
i\in[2n+1]\setminus\{n+k\}
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
is used only for generating
v_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
in
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0^{n},\beta)
,
Proposition

3.22
directly follows from the security of
\mathsf{PRG}
.
∎

Lemma

3.19
follows from the above propositions.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.15
.

Next, we prove
Lemma

3.15
.

Proof.

Since the proof of
Lemma

3.15
is very similar to that of
Lemma

3.14
, we only explain the difference. First, we define an experiment
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
that is similar to
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
except that
n+k
is replaced with
2n+1
. Then by almost the same argument as that in the proof of
Lemma

3.14
using
Lemma

3.7
, we only have to prove

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)=1]-\mathrm{Pr}[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2}}(\lambda,0^{n},\beta)=1]\bigg|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(93)

for all QPT
\mathpzc{B}
.
Its proof is also similar to that of
Lemma

3.19
.
We define
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
and
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2,b}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
similarly to
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
and
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k,b}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
except that
n+k
is replaced with
2n+1
. Then the computational indistinguishability between

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
and
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
and between
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2,b}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
and
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2}}(\lambda,0^{n},\beta)
immediately follow from the security of
\mathsf{PRG}
.
We argue the computational indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2,a}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
and
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{2,b}}(\lambda,\theta,\beta)
based on the security of
\mathsf{FE}
as follows.

Let
\mathsf{Good}
be the event that there is no
u
such that
\mathsf{PRG}(u)=v_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
for all
i\in[2n]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
.
We have
\Pr[\mathsf{Good}]\geq 1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
Similarly to the proof of
Proposition

3.21
,
it suffices to prove that whenever
\mathsf{Good}
occurs, we have

\displaystyle g[f]((V,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2n+1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{2n+1}),(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]})

(94)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g[f]((V,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2n},0^{\lambda},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{2n+1}),(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]})

(95)

for all key queries
f
and
(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}
.
Below, we prove
Equation

94
.
We consider the following two cases.

•

If
\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j})=v_{i,j,b_{i,j}}
holds for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
, then by the assumption that
\mathsf{Good}
occurs, we have
b_{i,j}=z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[2n]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
. Then we have

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}b_{i,j}=\begin{cases}m^{(0)}_{i}&\text{~if~}i\in[n]\\
m^{(1)}_{i-n}&\text{~if~}i\in[n+1,2n]\end{cases}.

(96)

Then the LHS of
Equation

94
is equal to
f(m^{(\gamma)})
where
\gamma=\beta_{2n+1}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}b_{2n+1,j}

and the RHS of
Equation

94
is equal to
f(m^{(\gamma^{\prime})})
where
\gamma^{\prime}=\beta_{2n+1}\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in[\lambda]}b_{2n+1,j}
.
By the restriction on
\mathpzc{B}
, we have
f(m^{(0)})=f(m^{(1)})
. Therefore, both sides of
Equation

94
are equal to
f(m^{(0)})=f(m^{(1)})
.

•

Otherwise, both sides of
Equation

94
are equal to
\bot
.

In either case,
Equation

94
holds. This completes the proof of
Lemma

3.15
.
∎

4
Bounded Collusion-Resistant Functional Encryption with Certified Everlasting Deletion

4.1
Definitions

We also require verification correctness with QOTP for
q
-bounded certified everlasting simulation-secure FE because we need it for the construction of certified everlasting secure FE in
Section

4.3
.

Definition 4.1 (Verification Correctness with QOTP).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
and a PPT algorithm
\mathsf{Recover}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert}^{*})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda},1^{q})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}\\
\widetilde{\mathsf{cert}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(Z^{b}X^{a}\mathsf{ct}X^{a}Z^{b})\\
\mathsf{cert}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Recover}(a,b,\widetilde{\mathsf{cert}})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Another is an adaptively simulation-based security notion in the bounded collusion-resistant setting. The other is a non-adaptively simulation-based security notion in the bounded collusion-resistant setting.
We consider only bounded collusion-resistance in the simulation-based definitions because achieving simulation-based security is impossible in the collusion-resistant setting
[
AGVW13
]
.

Our simulation-based security notion is a natural extension of that in the classical FE setting
[
GVW12
]
.
Note that the setup algorithm additionally takes
1^{q}
as input in the bounded collusion-resistant setting where
q
is the total number of key queries.

Definition 4.2 (
q
-Bounded Certified Everlasting Simulation-Security).

Let
q
be a polynomial of
\lambda
.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a
q
-bounded FE with certified everlasting deletion scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sim}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
and an unbounded adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.
Let
\mathpzc{Sim}_{1}
,
\mathpzc{Sim}_{2}
, and
\mathpzc{Sim}_{3}
be a QPT algorithm.

1.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda},1^{q})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
is allowed to make arbitrary key queries.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})

and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.
Let
q_{\mathsf{pre}}
be the number of times that
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
makes key queries in this phase.
Let
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{y_{i}\coloneqq f_{i}(m),f_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\}_{i\in[q_{\mathsf{pre}}]}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
chooses
m\in\mathcal{M}
and sends
m
to the challenger.

4.

The experiment works as follows:

•

If
b=0
, the challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
, and sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

•

If
b=1
, the challenger computes
(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{st}_{q_{\mathsf{pre}}})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Sim}_{1}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathcal{V},1^{|m|})
, and sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
, where
\mathsf{st}_{q_{\mathsf{pre}}}
is a quantum state.

5.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
is allowed to make arbitrary key queries at most
q-q_{\mathsf{pre}}
times.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger works as follows.

•

If
b=0
, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

•

If
b=1
, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
, computes
(\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}},\mathsf{st}_{\ell})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Sim}_{2}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell},f_{\ell}(m),\mathsf{st}_{\ell-1})
, and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
,
where
\mathsf{st}_{\ell}
is a quantum state.

6.

If
b=1
, the challenger runs
\mathsf{vk}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Sim}_{3}(\mathsf{st}_{q})
.

7.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger and its internal state to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.

8.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
. If the output is
\top
, then the challenger outputs
\top
, and sends
\mathsf{MSK}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.
Otherwise, the challenger outputs
\bot
, and sends
\bot
to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.

9.

\mathpzc{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. If the challenger outputs
\top
, the output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is
q
-bounded adaptively certified everlasting simulation-secure if there exists a
QPT
simulator
\mathpzc{Sim}=(\mathpzc{Sim}_{1},\mathpzc{Sim}_{2},\mathpzc{Sim}_{3})
such that for any
QPT
adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
and any unbounded adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sim}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expd{\Sigma,\qA}{cert}{ever}{ada}{sim}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expd{\Sigma,\qA}{cert}{ever}{ada}{sim}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(102)

Remark
4.3.

Note that
Definitions

4.4
and
4.2
were presented before the work by Bartusek and Khurana
[
BK23
]
appeared. Although we can define simulation-based definitions based on the definitions by Bartusek and Khurana, we leave our original simulation-based definitions as a concurrent and independent work. We also note that the challenger can omit sending
\mathsf{MSK}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
in
Definitions

4.2
and
4.4
in the public-key setting based on the results by Bartusek and Khurana
[
BK23
, Claim A.3 and A.4]
.

We can consider a non-adaptive variant of the definition above.

Definition 4.4 (
q
-Bounded Non-Adaptive Certified Everlasting Simulation-Security).

Let
q
be a polynomial of
\lambda
.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a
q
-bounded FE with certified everlasting deletion scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{noada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sim}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
and an unbounded adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
. Let
\mathpzc{Sim}
be a QPT algorithm.

1.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
is allowed to make arbitrary key queries.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})

and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.
Let
q
be the total number of times that
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
makes key queries.
Let
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{y_{i}\coloneqq f_{i}(m),f_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\}_{i\in[q]}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
chooses
m\in\mathcal{M}
and sends
m
to the challenger.

4.

The experiment works as follows:

•

If
b=0
, the challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
, and sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

•

If
b=1
, the challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Sim}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathcal{V},1^{|m|})
, and sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

5.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger and its internal state to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.

6.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
.
If the output is
\top
, then the challenger outputs
\top
, and sends
\mathsf{MSK}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.
Otherwise, the challenger outputs
\bot
, and sends
\bot
to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.

7.

\mathpzc{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. If the challenger outputs
\top
, the output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is
q
-bounded non-adaptive certified everlasting simulation-secure if there exists a
QPT
simulator
\mathpzc{Sim}
such that for any
QPT
adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
and any unbounded adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{noada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{sim}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expd{\Sigma,\qA}{cert}{ever}{noada}{sim}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expd{\Sigma,\qA}{cert}{ever}{noada}{sim}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(103)

We need to consider standard simulation-security notions for FE with certified everlasting deletion.
We note that the following two security definitions are simulation-based ones defined in
[
GVW12
]
.

Definition 4.5 (
q
-Bounded Non-Adaptive Simulation-Security for FE with Certified Everlasting Deletion

]
).

Let
q
be a polynomial of

.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
