Proof of
Proposition

4.22
.

In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
, the polynomials
\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{\ell}
are chosen with constant terms
x_{1},\cdots,x_{\ell}
, respectively.
In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
, these polynomials are now chosen with
0
constant terms.
This only affects the distribution of
\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{\ell}
themselves and polynomials
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{S}
.
Moreover, only the evaluations of these polynomials on the points in
\mathcal{L}
affect the outputs of the experiments.
Now observe that:

•

The distribution of the values
\{\mu_{1}(i),\cdots,\mu_{\ell}(i)\}_{i\in\mathcal{L}}
are identical to both
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
.
This is because in both experiments, we choose these polynomials to be random degree
t
polynomials (with different constraints in the constant term),
so their evaluation on the points in
\mathcal{L}
are identically distributed, since
|\mathcal{L}|\leq t
.

•

The values
\{\xi_{1}(i),\cdots,\xi_{S}(i)\}_{i\in\mathcal{L}}
depend only on the values
\{\mu_{1}(i),\cdots,\mu_{\ell}(i)\}_{i\in\mathcal{L}}
.

Proposition

4.22
follows from these observations.
∎

4.5
Discussion on
q
-Bounded Consturction for All Circuits

We discuss technical hurdles to achieve certified everlasting secure bounded collusion-resistant FE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
from standard PKE.

Gorbunov, Vaikuntanathan, and Wee
[
GVW12
]
presented a conversion from FE for
\mathsf{NC}^{1}
to FE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
by using randomized encoding or FHE. However, we cannot directly apply their techniques in the certified everlasting setting.
When we use randomized encoding, we use a functional decryption key for circuit
G_{f}
that takes
m
as an input and outputs a randomized encoding
\widetilde{f(m)}
.
22
22

22

For simplicity, we ignore how to set randomness for randomized encoding here since it is not an essential issue.

That is, we can obtain
\widetilde{f(m)}
(and
f(m)
via a decoding algorithm) from the functional decryption key and ciphertext of
m
since randomized encoding is computable in a constant-depth circuit
[
AIK06
]
.

The first problem is that even if we use
certified everlasting secure
FE for
\mathsf{NC}^{1}
, information about
m
remains in
\widetilde{f(m)}
since the decryption result does not directly provide
f(m)
. More specifically, adversaries can keep
\widetilde{f(m)}
(this is classical information) before deletion and an unbounded adversary could recover
m
from
\widetilde{f(m)}
even after
\mathsf{Enc}(m)
was deleted.

The second problem is that we cannot use certified everlasting secure randomized encoding to solve the first problem since we use FE for
classical
circuits here. In certified everlasting secure randomized encoding,
\widetilde{f(m)}
must be quantum state, which cannot be supported by FE for classical circuits. We do not have certified everlasting secure FE that supports quantum circuits computing quantum state. Moreover, we do not know how to achieve certified everlasting secure randomized encoding. Thus, the approach using randomized encoding does not work.

The approach using FHE also has problems. In this approach, we consider a functional decryption key for circuit
G_{f}
that takes an FHE ciphertext
\mathsf{fhe}.\mathsf{ct}
and an FHE decryption key
\mathsf{fhe}.\mathsf{sk}
and outputs
\mathsf{fhe}.\mathsf{ct}
and
\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{fhe}.\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Eval}(f,\mathsf{fhe}.\mathsf{ct}))
. Here, we must output
\mathsf{fhe}.\mathsf{ct}
as the public part because we use FE for
\mathsf{NC}^{1}
and need to apply
f\in\mathsf{P/poly}
by
\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Eval}
in the public part (though the FHE decryption part is in
\mathsf{NC}^{1}
).
23
23

23

See
[
GVW12
]
for the detail.
That is, the FHE part must be also certified everlasting secure.

First, we cannot use certified everlasting secure FHE in a black-box way. We need to encrypt an FHE ciphertext by FE for
\mathsf{NC}^{1}
in this approach. However, if FHE is certified everlasting secure, a ciphertext is quantum state, which is not supported by our certified everlasting secure FE for
\mathsf{NC}^{1}
.

Second, even if we use certified everlasting secure FHE in a non-black-box way like our compute-and-compare obfuscation construction in
Section

5.2
(by separating the classical FHE part from the BB84 state), the approach does not work due to the following reason. To achieve certified everlasting security,
\mathsf{fhe}.\mathsf{ct}
is an encryption of
m\oplus\bigoplus_{i}\theta_{i}
where
\theta
is a basis choice as in
Section

5.2
. To unmask
\bigoplus_{i}\theta_{i}
, we need to coherently apply
f
to
\mathsf{fhe}.\mathsf{ct}
and BB84 state as the certified everlasting secure FHE by Bartusek and Khurana
[
BK23
]
. However, we cannot execute the coherent evaluation in the FE decryption mechanism (cannot take BB84 state as input). Hence, we obtain
f(m\oplus\bigoplus_{i}\theta_{i})
and the correctness does not hold. Thus, the approach using FHE does not work too.

Another plausible (but failed) approach is using the framework by Ananth and Vaikuntanathan
[
AV19
]
. They constructed bounded collusion-resistant FE for
\mathsf{P/poly}

without the bootstrapping method
by Gorbunov et al.
[
GVW12
]
. However, their construction heavily relies on a secure multi-party computation protocol based on
PRG
. It is hard to define certified everlasting security for PRG because there is nothing to delete. Thus, it is unclear how to use their framework in the certified everlasting setting.

Therefore, previous approaches for converting FE for
\mathsf{NC}^{1}
to FE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
do not work in the certified everlasting setting.

5
Compute-and-Compare Obfuscation with Certified Everlasting Deletion

5.1
Definition

In this section, we introduce the notion of compute-and-compare obfuscation with certified everlasting security.

Definition 5.1 (Compute-and-Compare Obfuscation with Certified Everlasting Deletion (Syntax)).

A compute-and-compare obfuscation with certified everlasting deletion is a tuple of algorithms
(\mathpzc{CCObf},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
for the family of distributions
D=\{D_{\mathsf{param}}\}_{\mathsf{param}}
and message space
\mathcal{M}
.

\mathpzc{CCObf}(1^{\lambda},P,\mathsf{lock},m)
:

The obfuscation algorithm takes as input a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, a circuit
P
, a lock string
\mathsf{lock}\in\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}
and a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs an obfuscated circuit
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}
and a verification key
\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm takes as input an obfuscated circuit
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}
and outputs a classical certificate
\mathsf{cert}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top\textbf{ or }\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes as input the verification key
\mathsf{vk}
and a certificate
\mathsf{cert}
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Definition 5.2 (Correctness of Compute-and-Compare Obfuscation with Certified Everlasting Deletion).

The correctness of compute-and-compare obfuscation with certified everlasting deletion for the family of distributions
D=\{D_{\mathsf{param}}\}_{\mathsf{param}}
and message space
\mathcal{M}
is defined as follows.

Functionality Preserving:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for all circuit
P
, all lock value
\mathsf{lock}
, and all message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr[\forall x,\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}(x)=\mathbf{CC}[P,\mathsf{lock},m](x)\mid\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{CCObf}(1^{\lambda},P,\mathsf{lock},m)]\geq 1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(134)

Verification Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for all circuit
P
, all lock value
\mathsf{lock}
, and all message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\neq\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{CCObf}(1^{\lambda},P,\mathsf{lock},m)\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}})\\
\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition 5.3 (Certified Everlasting Security of Compute-and-Compare Obfuscation).

Let
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}=(\mathpzc{CCObf},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a compute-and-compare obfuscation with certified everlasting deletion for the family of distributions
D=\{D_{\mathsf{param}}\}_{\mathsf{param}}
and a message space
\mathcal{M}
. We consider experiments
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,b)
and
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,b)
played between a challenger and a non-uniform QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}=\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda},\ket{\psi}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
. Let
\mathpzc{Sim}
be a QPT algorithm. The experiments are defined as follows:

1.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}(\ket{\psi}_{\lambda})
submits a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger chooses
(P,\mathsf{lock},\mathpzc{aux})\leftarrow D_{\mathsf{param}}
.

3.

The challenger computes
(\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}^{(0)},\mathsf{vk}^{(0)})\leftarrow\mathpzc{CCObf}(1^{\lambda},P,\mathsf{lock},m)
or
(\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}^{(1)},\mathsf{vk}^{(1)})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{P},1^{|m|})
and sends
(\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}^{(b)},\mathpzc{aux})
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
according to the bit
b
. Recall that a program
P
has an associated set of parameters
\mathsf{pp}_{P}
(input size, output size, circuit size) which we do not need to hide.

4.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
submits a certificate of deletion
\mathsf{cert}
and its internal state
\rho
to the challenger.

5.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{(b)},\mathsf{cert})
. If the outcome is
\top
, the experiment
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,b)
outputs
\rho
; otherwise if the outcome is
\bot
then
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,b)
outputs
\bot
and ends.

6.

The challenger sends the outcome of
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{(b)},\mathsf{cert})
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
.

7.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
outputs its guess
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
which is the output of the experiment
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,b)
.

We say that the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}
is certified everlasting secure if for any non-uniform QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}=\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda},\ket{\psi}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,0),\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda),

(137)

and

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(138)

5.2
Construction

In this section, we construct a compute-and-compare obfuscation with certified everlasting deletion from classical compute-and-compare obfuscation and FHE.

Ingredients.

We use the following building blocks.

1.

\Sigma_{\mathsf{fhe}}=\mathsf{FHE}.(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Dec})
be a classical FHE scheme.

2.

\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}=\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}
be a classical compute-and-compare obfuscation scheme.

Certified everlasting compute-and-compare obfuscation for multi-bit message.

We construct
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}}=(\mathpzc{CCObf},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
for the family of distribution
D=\{D_{\mathsf{param}}\}_{\mathsf{param}}
and message space
\mathcal{M}
. We let the message space
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{n}
.

\mathpzc{CCObf}(1^{\lambda},P,\mathsf{lock},m)
:

1.

Sample
R\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

2.

Sample
(\mathsf{fpk},\mathsf{fsk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

Compute
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{fDec},R,1)
where
\mathsf{fDec}(\cdot)=\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{fsk},\cdot)
.

4.

Compute
\widetilde{I}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},I,\mathsf{lock},R)
where
I(X)=X
for every
X
.

5.

Represent
(P\|\widetilde{I})=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

6.

Sample
\bm{\theta}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

7.

Set
\widetilde{b}_{i}\coloneqq b_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

8.

Denote
m=(m_{1},\dots,m_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

9.

Sample
\bm{\theta}_{\ell+k},\bm{z}_{\ell+k}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for all
k\in[n]
.

10.

Set
\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}\coloneqq m_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{\ell+k,j}=0}z_{\ell+k,j}
for all
k\in[n]
.

11.

Compute
\mathsf{fct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fpk},(\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{b}_{i}))
for all
i\in[\ell+n]
.

12.

Output
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}\coloneqq(\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}},\{(\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}},\mathsf{fct}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+n]},\mathsf{fpk})
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq(\{\bm{z}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell+n]})
.

How to evaluate
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}(x)
:

1.

Parse
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}\coloneqq(\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}},\{(\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}},\mathsf{fct}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+n]},\mathsf{fpk})
.

2.

Define a circuit
\widehat{U}_{x}
as in Figure
2
.

3.

To compute an evaluated ciphertext for
\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{fpk},\widehat{U}_{x},\cdot)
, apply
\widehat{U}_{x}
homomorphically in superposition with the input
(\{(\ket{\bm{z}_i}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}},\mathsf{fct}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell]},(\ket{\bm{z}_{\ell+k}}_{\bm{\theta}_{\ell+k}},\mathsf{fct}_{\ell+k}))
and obtain a ciphertext
\ket{\fct_{\ell+k, P}}
for each
k\in[n]
.

4.

Apply
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}(\cdot)
in superposition with the input
\ket{\fct_{\ell+k, P}}
and measure the output register in the standard basis to get a classical outcome
\beta_{k}
for each
k\in[n]
.

5.

Set
m_{k}=1
if
\beta_{k}=1
, else set
m_{k}=0
, for each
k\in[n]
.

6.

Output
m=(m_{1},\dots,m_{n})
.

Circuit
\widehat{U}_{x}

Hardwire:

x

Input:

(\{(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{b}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell]},(\bm{z}_{\ell+k},\bm{\theta}_{\ell+k},\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}))

1.

Compute
b_{i}\coloneqq\widetilde{b}_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

2.

Reconstruct
(C\|\widetilde{I})
from
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})
.

3.

Compute
m_{k}\coloneqq\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{\ell+k,j}=0}z_{\ell+k,j}
.

4.

Output
m_{k}\cdot\widetilde{I}(C(x))

Figure 2:
The description of the circuit
\widehat{U}_{x}

\mathpzc{Del}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}})
:

1.

Parse
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}\coloneqq(\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}},\{(\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}},\mathsf{fct}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+n]},\mathsf{fpk})
.

2.

Measure
\ket{\bm{z}_i}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}}
in the Hadamard basis for all
i\in[\ell+n]
, and obtain
(\bm{z}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\ell+n}^{\prime})
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq(\bm{z}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\ell+n}^{\prime})
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{vk}=(\{(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+n]})
and
\mathsf{cert}=(\bm{z}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\ell+n}^{\prime})
.

2.

If
\left((z_{i,j}=z_{i,j}^{\prime})\wedge(\theta_{i,j}=1)\right)
holds for all
i\in[\ell+n]
and
j\in[\lambda]
, then output
\top
; otherwise output
\bot
.

Security.

We use
Lemma

3.5
by Bartusek and Khurana
[
BK23
]
to prove the security of our construction.

Theorem 5.4.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}
is a secure compute-and-compare obfuscation and
\Sigma_{\textsf{fhe}}
is an IND-CPA secure fully homomorphic encryption then
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}}
is a certified everlasting secure compute-and-compare obfuscation scheme for the family of distribution
D=\{D_{\mathsf{param}}\}_{\mathsf{param}}
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.4
.

We first describe the simulator of
\mathpzc{CCObf}
, denoted as
\mathpzc{Sim}
, before we go to the formal security analysis. Let
\mathsf{CCO}.\mathsf{Sim}
be the simulator the classical compute-and-compare obfuscation employed as a building block in the above construction. For
(P,\mathsf{lock},\mathpzc{aux})\leftarrow D_{\mathsf{param}}
, the algorithm
\mathpzc{Sim}
works as follows:

\mathpzc{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{P},1^{n})
:

1.

Sample
R\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

2.

Sample
(\mathsf{fpk},\mathsf{fsk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

Compute
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{fDec},R,1)
.

4.

Sample
\bm{\theta}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for all
i\in[\ell+n]
.

5.

Set
\widetilde{b}_{i}\coloneqq 0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[\ell+n]
.

6.

Compute
\mathsf{fct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fpk},(\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{b}_{i}))
for all
i\in[\ell+n]
.

7.

Output
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}\coloneqq(\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}},\{(\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}},\mathsf{fct}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+n]},\mathsf{fpk})
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq(\{\bm{z}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell+n]})
.

Note that
\mathpzc{Sim}
does not need information about
(P,\mathsf{lock},\mathpzc{aux})
except
\mathsf{pp}_{P}
. We show that

\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,0),\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(139)

using Lemma
3.5
and the post-quantum security of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{fhe}}
. We use the following sequence of hybrids to prove this.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,0)
. Let
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}^{(0)}\coloneqq(\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}},\{(\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}},\mathsf{fct}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+n]},\mathsf{fpk})
be the compute-and-compare obfuscated circuit computed using the honest
\mathpzc{CCObf}
algorithm.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This hybrid works as follows:

1.

\mathpzc{A}
submits a message
m\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger chooses
(P,\mathsf{lock},\mathpzc{aux})\leftarrow D_{\mathsf{param}}
.

3.

The challenger computes the obfuscated circuit as follows:

(a)

Sample
(\mathsf{fpk},\mathsf{fsk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
and
R\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

(b)

Compute
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{fDec},R,1)
.

(c)

Sample
\bm{\theta}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for all
i\in[\ell+n]
.

(d)

Set
\widetilde{b}_{i}\coloneqq 0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
for
i\in[\ell]
.

(e)

Set
\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}\coloneqq m_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{\ell+k,j}=0}z_{\ell+k,j}
for all
k\in[n]
.

(f)

Compute
\mathsf{fct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fpk},(\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{b}_{i}))
for all
i\in[\ell+n]
.

(g)

Set
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}\coloneqq(\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}},\{(\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}},\mathsf{fct}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+n]},\mathsf{fpk})
.

The challenge sends
\widetilde{\mathpzc{P}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
sends a deletion certificate
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq(\bm{z}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\ell+n}^{\prime})
and its internal state
\rho
to the challenger.

5.

The challenger checks if
\left((z_{i,j}=z_{i,j}^{\prime})\wedge(\theta_{i,j}=1)\right)
holds for all
i\in[\ell+n]
and
j\in[\lambda]
. If the check fails, the experiment halts and returns
\bot
; otherwise, go to the next step.

6.

The experiment outputs
\rho
as a final output.

Note that, the FHE ciphertexts
\{\mathsf{fct}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]}
contain no information about the lock value
\mathsf{lock}
, the random string
R
and the circuit
P
. To prove the indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, we consider a sequence of intermediate hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
for
k\in[0,\ell]
where
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,0}
is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and the only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
is that
\mathsf{fct}_{k}
is an encryption of
(\bm{\theta}_{k},b_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{k,j}=0}z_{k,j})
where
b_{k}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
is the same as
b_{k}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
b_{k}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
is set to zero for
k\in[\ell]
.

Now, we consider a sequence of experiments
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},\beta)
for
k\in[\ell]
between a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
for
\bm{\theta}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and
\beta\in\{0,1\}
. The experiment
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},\beta)
is basically the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
where we take
\bm{\theta}_{k}=\bm{\theta}
,
\widetilde{b}_{k}=\beta
and
\mathpzc{B}
plays the role of
\mathpzc{A}
,
\mathpzc{C}
plays the role of the challenger. In particular, it works as follows:

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},\beta)
:

1.

\mathpzc{B}
submits a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger chooses
(P,\mathsf{lock},\mathpzc{aux})\leftarrow D_{\mathsf{param}}
.

3.

The challenger computes the obfuscated circuit as follows:

(a)

Sample
(\mathsf{fpk},\mathsf{fsk})\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
and
R\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

(b)

Compute
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{fDec},R,1)
.

(c)

Compute
\widetilde{I}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},I,\mathsf{lock},R)
.

(d)

Represent
(P\|\widetilde{I})=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

(e)

Sample
\bm{\theta}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for all
i\in[\ell+n]\setminus\{k\}
.

(f)

Set
\widetilde{b}_{i}
for
i\in[\ell]
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[1,k-1]\\
\beta&\text{if~}i=k\\
b_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[k+1,\ell]\end{cases}.

(140)

(g)

Set
\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}\coloneqq m_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{\ell+k,j}=0}z_{\ell+k,j}
for all
k\in[n]
.

(h)

Compute
\mathsf{fct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fpk},(\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{b}_{i}))
for all
i\in[\ell+n]
where
\bm{\theta}_{k}\coloneqq\bm{\theta}
.

The challenge sends
(\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}},\{(\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}},\mathsf{fct}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell+n]\setminus\{k\}},\mathsf{fct}_{k},\mathsf{fpk})
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

4.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

Let us define
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}(\bm{\theta})=\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},\beta)
. We first show that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}(\bm{\theta})=1]-\Pr[\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}(\bm{0}_{\lambda})=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(141)

\mathcal{Z}^{k,1}_{\lambda}
:

This is exactly the same as
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}(\bm{\theta})
except the challenger uses the bits of
\widetilde{I}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{I},1^{\absolutevalue{R}})
instead of
\widetilde{I}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},I,\mathsf{lock},R)
to set
\widetilde{b}_{i}
for all
i\in[k+1,\ell]
. The indistinguishability between the distributions
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}(\bm{\theta})
and
\mathcal{Z}^{k,1}_{\lambda}
follows from the post-quantum security of the classical compute-and-compare obfuscation scheme
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}
.

\mathcal{Z}^{k,2}_{\lambda}
:

This is exactly the same as
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,1}
except the challenger replaces
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{fDec},R,1)
with the simulated obfuscated circuit
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{\mathsf{fDec}},1^{1})
. The indistinguishability between the distributions
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,1}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{k,2}_{\lambda}
follows from the post-quantum security of the classical compute-and-compare scheme
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}
.

\mathcal{Z}^{k,3}_{\lambda}
:

This is exactly the same as
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,2}
except the challenger computes
\mathsf{fct}_{k}\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fpk},(\bm{0}_{\lambda},\widetilde{b}_{k}))
instead of encrypting
(\bm{\theta},\widetilde{b}_{k})
. The indistinguishability between the distributions
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,2}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{k,3}_{\lambda}
follows from the post-quantum security of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{fhe}}
.

\mathcal{Z}^{k,4}_{\lambda}
:

This is exactly the same as
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,3}
except the challenger replaces
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{\mathsf{fDec}},1^{1})
with the real obfuscated circuit
\widetilde{\mathsf{fDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{fDec},R,1)
. The indistinguishability between the distributions
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,3}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{k,4}_{\lambda}
follows from the post-quantum security of the classical compute-and-compare obfuscation scheme
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}
.

\mathcal{Z}^{k,5}_{\lambda}
:

This is exactly the same as
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,4}
except the challenger uses the bits of
\widetilde{I}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Obf}(1^{\lambda},I,\mathsf{lock},R)
instead of
\widetilde{I}\leftarrow\mathsf{CC}.\mathsf{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{I},1^{1})
to set
\widetilde{b}_{i}
for all
i\in[k+1,\ell]
. The indistinguishability between the distributions
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,4}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{k,5}_{\lambda}
follows from the post-quantum security of the classical compute-and-compare obfuscation scheme
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CCO}}
.

Observe that, the distributions
\mathcal{Z}^{k,5}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k}(\bm{0}_{\lambda})
are identical. Hence,
Equation

141
holds for all
k\in[\ell]
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.5
, for any (unbounded) adversary
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0),\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(142)

where the experiment
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lambda}^{k}(b)=\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,b)
works as follows:

\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,b):

1.

Sample
\bm{z},\bm{\theta}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
receives
(1^{\lambda},\ket{\bm{z}}_{\bm{\theta}})
as input.

3.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
interacts with
\mathpzc{C}
as in
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},b\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j})
where
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
plays the role of
\mathpzc{B}
.

4.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
outputs a string
\bm{z}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and a quantum state
\rho
.

5.

If
z_{j}=z_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{j}=1
then the experiment outputs
\rho
, and otherwise it outputs a special symbol
\bot
.

Note that the only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
is that
\widetilde{b}_{k}
is set to be
b_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
. Let us assume
b_{k}=1
, since otherwise
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
are identical. We construct
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
that distinguishes
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0)
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,1)
if
\mathpzc{A}
distinguishes between the hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\bm{z}}_{\bm{\theta}})
:

1.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
plays the role of
\mathpzc{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
where the external challenger
\mathpzc{C}
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,b)
is used to simulate the challenger of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
.
\mathpzc{C}
sends the obfuscated circuit to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

Suppose
\mathpzc{A}
sends a certificate
\mathsf{cert}=(\bm{z}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\ell}^{\prime})
to the challenger where
\bm{z}_{i}^{\prime}=(z_{i,j}^{\prime})_{j\in[\lambda]}
for all
i\in[\ell]
. Then,
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
sets
\bm{z}^{\prime}\coloneqq\bm{z}_{k}^{\prime}
.

3.

Outputs
\bm{z}^{\prime}
and the internal state
\rho
of
\mathpzc{A}
.

We observe that
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
if
b=1
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
if
b=0
(since we are assuming
b_{k}=1
). Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k})\leq\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lambda}^{k}(0),\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{\lambda}^{k}(1)).

(143)

Combining
Equations

142
and
143
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k})\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(144)

Recall that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,0}\equiv\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,\ell}\equiv\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. Therefore, combining the advantages of
\mathpzc{A}
in the sequence of intermediate hybrids as obtained in Equation
144
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{0},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1})\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(145)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

This is exactly the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except the fact that instead of encrypting the challenge message
m\in\{0,1\}^{n}
the FHE ciphertexts
\{\mathsf{fct}_{\ell+k}\}_{k\in[n]}
are encrypted to the message
\bm{0}_{n}
. More precisely, the challenger samples
\bm{\theta}_{\ell+k},\bm{z}_{\ell+k}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and sets
\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}\coloneqq 0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{\ell+k,j}=0}z_{\ell+k,j}
for all
k\in[n]
instead of setting
\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}\coloneqq m_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{\ell+k,j}=0}z_{\ell+k,j}
. Finally, it obtains
\mathsf{fct}_{\ell+k}\leftarrow\mathsf{FHE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fpk},(\bm{\theta}_{\ell+k},\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}))
for all
k\in[n]
where the encrypted bits
\{\widetilde{b}_{\ell+k}\}_{k\in[n]}
contain no information about the message
m
. Since the FHE master secret key
\mathsf{fsk}
is not required to simulate the hybrids, the indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
is guaranteed by the post-quantum semantic security of FHE. We can follow a similar argument as in the previous hybrid and show that

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{2})\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(146)

We observe that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,1)
. Therefore, by combing the advantages of
\mathpzc{A}
in the consecutive hybrids and applying the triangular inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,0),\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(147)

Finally, it is easy to show the computational indistinguishability, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sim}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ccobf}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(148)

using the security of FHE and the post-quantum security of
\mathsf{CCO}
.
We skip the formal description as it follows from the similar sequence of hybrids that we used to establish
Equation

141
except that
\mathsf{fct}_{k}
is changed from encryption of
(\bm{\theta},\widetilde{b}_{k})
to
(\bm{\theta},0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{k,j}=0}z_{k,j})
(instead of changing it from
(\bm{\theta},\widetilde{b}_{k})
to
(\bm{0}_{\lambda},\widetilde{b}_{k})
in
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}^{k,3}
). This completes the proof.
∎

6
Predicate Encryption with Certified Everlastng Deletion

6.1
Definition

We describe the notion of PE with certified everlasting deletion which generates a quantum ciphertext that can be deleted when required and the deletion is verified using a classical certificate of deletion.

Definition 6.1 (PE with Ceritifed Everlasting Deletion (Syntax)).

A certified everlasting PE is tuple of QPT algorithms
(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
with a class predicates
\mathcal{P}
, a class of attributes
\mathcal{X}
and a message space
\mathcal{M}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})
:

The parameter setup algorithm takes as input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
and outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{P})
:

The key generation algorithm takes as input the master secret key
\mathsf{msk}
and a predicate
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}
, and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{P}}
corresponding to the predicate
\mathsf{P}
.

\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x,m)\rightarrow(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk})
:

The encryption algorithm takes as input the public key
\mathsf{pk}
, an attribute
x\in\mathcal{X}
and a message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, and outputs a quantum ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}
and a classical verification key
\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow m^{\prime}\textbf{ or }\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{\textsf{P}}
and a quantum ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}
, and outputs a classical plaintext
m^{\prime}
or
\bot
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm takes as input the ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}
and outputs a classical certificate
\mathsf{cert}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top\textbf{ or }\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes as input the verification key
\mathsf{vk}
and a certificate
\mathsf{cert}
, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Definition 6.2 (Correctness of PE with Certified Everlasting Deletion).

The correctness of PE with certified deletion for a class of predicates
\mathcal{P}
is defined as follows.

Decryption correctness:

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N},\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P},x\in\mathcal{X},m\in\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathsf{P}(x)=1
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathpzc{ct})\neq m\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{P}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{P})\\
(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification correctness:

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N},x\in\mathcal{X},m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\neq\top\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pp},x,m)\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition 6.3 (Certified Everlasting Security of PE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a PE with certified everlasting deletion for a class of predicates
\mathcal{P}
, a class of attributes
\mathcal{X}
and a message space
\mathcal{M}
. We consider two experiments
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
and
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
played between a challenger and and a non-uniform QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}=\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda},\ket{\psi}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
. The experiments are defined as follows:

1.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}(\ket{\psi}_{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
sends
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}
to the challenger and receives
\mathsf{sk}_{\textsf{P}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{P})
from the challenger.

3.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
sends a pair of challenge attributes
(x_{0},x_{1})
and a pair of challenge messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
satisfying the fact that
\mathsf{P}(x_{0})=\mathsf{P}(x_{1})=0
for all
\mathsf{P}
queried so far in the key query phase.

4.

The challenger computes
(\mathpzc{ct}_{b},\mathsf{vk}_{b})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x_{b},m_{b})
and sends
\mathpzc{ct}_{b}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
can make further key queries with
P
satisfying
\mathsf{P}(x_{0})=\mathsf{P}(x_{1})=0
.

6.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
sends a certificate of deletion
\mathsf{cert}
and its internal state
\rho
to the challenger.

7.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{b},\mathsf{cert})
. If the outcome is
\top
, the experiment
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
outputs
\rho
; otherwise if the outcome is
\bot
then
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
output
\bot
and ends.

8.

The challenger sends the outcome of
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{(b)},\mathsf{cert})
to
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
.

9.

Again,
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
can make key queries with polynomial number of policies
P
satisfying
\mathsf{P}(x_{0})=\mathsf{P}(x_{1})=0
.

10.

\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
outputs its guess
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
which is the output of the experiment
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
.

We say that the
\Sigma
is adaptively certified everlasting secure if for any non-uniform QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}=\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda},\ket{\psi}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0),\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda),

(155)

and

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(156)

We can define similar experiment
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
and
\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
where
\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda}
is restricted to submit the challenge attributes
x_{0},x_{1}
before it receives
\mathsf{pk}
from the challenger. We say that the
\Sigma
is selectively certified everlasting secure if for any non-uniform QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}=\{\mathpzc{A}_{\lambda},\ket{\psi}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0),\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda),

(157)

and

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(158)

6.2
Construction

In this section, we construct a PE with certified everlasting deletion from a compute-and-compare obfuscation wiht certified everlasting deletion introduced in
Section

5
and a classical ABE.

Ingredients.

We use the following building blocks.

1.

\Sigma_{\textsf{abe}}=\mathsf{ABE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a classical ABE scheme for a class of predicates
\mathcal{P}
and message space
\mathcal{M}_{\textsf{abe}}=\{0,1\}^{\lambda+1}
.

2.

\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}}=\mathsf{CCO}.(\mathpzc{Obf},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a compute-and-compare obfuscation with certified everlasting deletion for a message space
\mathcal{M}_{\textsf{pe}}
and the family of distributions
D=\{D_{\mathsf{apk},x,\{\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i},\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i}}\}_{{\mathsf{apk},x,\{\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i},\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i}}}
.

Let
D=\{D_{\mathsf{apk},x,\{\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i},\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i}}\}_{{\mathsf{apk},x,\{\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i},\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i}}}
be a family of distributions where
D_{\mathsf{apk},x,\{\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i},\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i}}
outputs
(\mathsf{aDec},\mathsf{lock},\mathpzc{aux})
generated as follows.

•

Generate
\mathsf{act}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{apk},x,(\bm{\theta}_{i},\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}))
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

•

Construct
\mathsf{aDec}
described in
Figure

3
.

•

Choose
\mathsf{lock}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}=\mathcal{K}
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{aDec},\mathsf{lock},\mathpzc{aux}\coloneqq\bot)
.

PE with certified everlasting deletion.

We construct
\Sigma_{\textsf{pe-ce}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
for a class of predicates
\mathcal{P}
, a class of attributes
\mathcal{X}
and a message space
\mathcal{M}_{\textsf{pe}}
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})

1.

Sample
(\mathsf{apk},\mathsf{amsk})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Output
(\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\mathsf{apk},\mathsf{msk}\coloneqq\mathsf{amsk})
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},P)

1.

Parse
\mathsf{msk}=\mathsf{amsk}
.

2.

Compute
\mathsf{sk}_{P}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{amsk},P)
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{sk}_{P}
.

\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{apk},x,m)

1.

Parse
\mathsf{pk}=\mathsf{apk}
.

2.

Sample
R\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and denote
R=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{\ell})
.

3.

Sample
\bm{\theta}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

4.

Set
\widetilde{r}_{i}\coloneqq r_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

5.

Compute
\mathsf{act}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{apk},x,(\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{r}_{i}))
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

6.

(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}},\mathsf{vk}_{\mathsf{aDec}})\leftarrow\mathsf{CCO}.\mathpzc{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{aDec},R,m)
where
\mathsf{aDec}
is defined in Figure
3
.

7.

Output
\mathpzc{ct}\coloneqq(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}},\{\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]})
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq(\{(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathsf{vk}_{\mathsf{aDec}})
.

Hardwire:

\{\mathsf{act}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]}

Input:

(\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathsf{sk}_{P})

1.

Compute
(\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{r}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{P},\mathsf{act}_{i})
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

2.

Compute
r_{i}=\widetilde{r}_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

3.

Set
R\coloneqq(r_{1},\ldots,r_{\ell})

4.

Output
R

Figure 3:
The description of the circuit
\mathsf{aDec}

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{P},\mathpzc{ct})

1.

Parse
\mathpzc{ct}=(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}},\{\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]})
.

2.

Apply
\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}}
in superposition to the input
(\{\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathsf{sk}_{P})
and measure the output register to obtain
m^{\prime}
.

3.

Output
m^{\prime}
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})

1.

Parse
\mathpzc{ct}=(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}},\{\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]})
.

2.

Measure
\ket{\bm{z}_i}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}}
in the Hadamard basis for all
i\in[\ell]
and obtain
\bm{z}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\bm{z}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\ell}^{\prime})
.

3.

Compute
\mathsf{cert}_{\mathsf{aDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CCO}.\mathpzc{Del}(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}})
.

4.

Output
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq(\bm{z}^{\prime},\mathsf{cert}_{\mathsf{aDec}})
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})

1.

Parse
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq(\{(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathsf{vk}_{\mathsf{aDec}})
and
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq(\bm{z}^{\prime},\mathsf{cert}_{\mathsf{aDec}})
.

2.

If
(z_{i,j}=z_{i,j}^{\prime})\wedge(\theta_{i,j}=1)
holds for all
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[\lambda]
and
\mathsf{CCO}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{\mathsf{aDec}},\mathsf{cert}_{\mathsf{aDec}})=\top
, then output
\top
; otherwise output
\bot
.

Theorem 6.4.

If
\Sigma_{\mathsf{CECCO}}
is a certified everlasting secure compute-and-compare obfuscation for a message space
\mathcal{M}_{\textsf{pe}}
and the family of distributions
D=\{D_{\mathsf{apk},x,\{\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i},\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i}}\}_{{\mathsf{apk},x,\{\bm{\theta}_{i}\}_{i},\{\bm{z}_{i}\}_{i}}}
and
\Sigma_{\textsf{abe}}
is an adaptively (resp. selectively) secure ABE for a class of predicates
\mathcal{P}
, then
\Sigma_{\textsf{pe-ce}}
is an adaptively (resp. selectively) certified everlasting secure predicate encryption scheme for the class of predicates
\mathcal{P}
, message space
\mathcal{M}_{\textsf{pe}}
.

We focus on the case of adaptive security.

Proof.

To prove the theorem we consider an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
against the certified everlasting security of
\Sigma_{\textsf{pe-ce}}
. We consider the following sequence of hybrids.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}:

This is the original certified everlasting security experiment where the challenge bit is set to 0 (
\mathsf{EV}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\textsf{pe-ce}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0)
). More precisely, it works as follows:

1.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{apk},\mathsf{amsk})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\mathsf{apk}
, and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

The adversary
\mathpzc{A}
sends any polynomial number of secret key queries for
P\in\mathcal{P}
at any point of the experiment. The challenger generates
\mathsf{sk}_{P}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{amsk},P)
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{P}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends a pair of challenge attributes
(x_{0},x_{1})
and a pair of challenge messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
satisfying the fact that
P(x_{0})=P(x_{1})=0
for all
P
queried so far in the key query phase.

4.

The challenger computes the challenge ciphertext as follows:

(a)

Sample
R^{*}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{\ell})\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

(b)

Sample
\bm{\theta}_{i},\bm{z}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for all
i\in[\ell]
where
\bm{\theta}_{i}=(\theta_{i,j})_{j\in[\lambda]}
and
\bm{z}_{i}=(z_{i,j})_{j\in[\lambda]}
.

(c)

Set
\widetilde{r}_{i}\coloneqq r_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

(d)

Compute
\mathsf{act}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{apk},x_{0},(\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{r}_{i}))
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

(e)

(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}},\mathsf{vk}_{\mathsf{aDec}})\leftarrow\mathsf{CCO}.\mathpzc{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{aDec},R^{*},m_{0})
where
\mathsf{aDec}
is defined in Figure
3
.

(f)

Set
\mathpzc{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}},\{\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]})
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq(\{(\bm{z}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{i})\}_{i\in[\ell]},\mathsf{vk}_{\mathsf{aDec}})
.

The challenger sends
\mathpzc{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
sends a certificate
\mathsf{cert}=(\bm{z}^{\prime}=(\bm{z}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\ell}^{\prime}),\mathsf{cert}_{\mathsf{aDec}})
and its internal state
\rho
to the challenger where
\bm{z}_{i}^{\prime}=(z_{i,j}^{\prime})_{j\in[\lambda]}
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

6.

The challenger checks if
(z_{i,j}=z_{i,j}^{\prime})\wedge(\theta_{i,j}=1)
holds for all
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[\lambda]
and
\mathsf{CCO}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{\mathsf{aDec}},\mathsf{cert}_{\mathsf{aDec}})=\top
. If it does not hold, the challenger outputs
\bot
as the final output of the experiment. Otherwise, go to the next step.

7.

The experiment outputs
\rho
as a final output.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}:

This hybrid proceeds exactly similar to
\textsf{Hybd}_{0}
except that the ABE ciphertexts
\mathsf{act}_{i}
is now replaced with encryption of zero string. In particular, the hardwired values of
\mathsf{aDec}
are computed as
\mathsf{act}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{apk},x_{0},(\bm{\theta}_{i},0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}))
for all
i\in[\ell]
.

To prove the indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, we consider a sequence of intermediate hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
for
k\in[\ell]
where we take
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,0}
is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and the only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
is that
\mathsf{act}_{k}
is an encryption of
(\bm{\theta}_{k},r_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{k,j}=0}z_{k,j})
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
whereas it is an encryption of
(\bm{\theta}_{k},0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{k,j}=0}z_{k,j})
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
.

Now, we consider a sequence of experiments
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},\beta)
for
k\in[\ell]
between a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{B}
and a challenger
\mathpzc{C}
for
\bm{\theta}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and
\beta\in\{0,1\}
. The experiment
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,0}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},\beta)
is basically the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
where
\mathpzc{B}
plays the role of
\mathpzc{A}
and
\mathpzc{C}
plays the role of the challenger. In particular, it works as follows:

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},\beta):

1.

\mathpzc{C}
computes
(\mathsf{apk},\mathsf{amsk})\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
\mathsf{pk}\coloneqq\mathsf{apk}
, and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}
sends any polynomial number of secret key queries for
P\in\mathcal{P}
at any point of the experiment and
\mathpzc{C}
generates
\mathsf{sk}_{P}\leftarrow\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{amsk},P)
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{P}
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

3.

\mathpzc{B}
sends a pair of challenge attributes
(x_{0},x_{1})
and a pair of challenge messages
(m_{0},m_{1})
satisfying the fact that
P(x_{0})=P(x_{1})=0
for all
P
queried so far in the key query phase.

4.

\mathpzc{C}
computes the challenge ciphertext as follows:

(a)

Sample
R^{*}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{\ell})\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

(b)

Sample
\bm{z}_{i},\bm{\theta}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for all
i\in[\ell]\setminus\{k\}
where
\bm{\theta}_{i}=(\theta_{i,j})_{j\in[\lambda]}
and
\bm{z}_{i}=(z_{i,j})_{j\in[\lambda]}
.

(c)

Set
\widetilde{r}_{i}
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[1,k-1]\\
\beta&\text{if~}i=k\\
r_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[k+1,\ell]\end{cases}.

(159)

(d)

Compute
\mathsf{act}_{i}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{act}_{i}\leftarrow\begin{cases}\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{apk},x_{0},(\bm{\theta},\widetilde{r}_{k}))&\text{if~}i=k\\
\mathsf{ABE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{apk},x_{0},(\bm{\theta}_{i},\widetilde{r}_{i}))&\text{if~}i\in[\ell]\setminus\{k\}\\
\end{cases}.

(160)

(e)

(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}},\mathsf{vk}_{\mathsf{aDec}})\leftarrow\mathsf{CCO}.\mathpzc{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{aDec},R^{*},m_{0})
where
\mathsf{aDec}
is defined in Figure
3
.

The challenger sends
(\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}},\{\ket{\bm{z}_{i}}_{\bm{\theta}_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]\setminus\{k\}})
to
\mathpzc{B}
.

5.

\mathpzc{B}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

Since all the secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{P}
corresponding to predicates
P
queried by the adversary satisfy the condition that
P(x_{0})=0
, the semantic security of ABE ensures that

\begin{array}[]{c}\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},\beta)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{0}_{\lambda},\beta)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).\end{array}

Therefore, by Lemma
3.7
, for any QPT (unbounded) adversary
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
, we have

\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0),\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,1))\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)

(161)

where the experiment
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,b)
works as follows:

\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,b):

1.

Sample
\bm{z},\bm{\theta}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
takes
(1^{\lambda},\ket{\bm{z}}_{\bm{\theta}})
as input.

3.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
interacts with
\mathpzc{C}
as in
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathpzc{B},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,\bm{\theta},b\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j})
where
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
plays the role of
\mathpzc{B}
.

4.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
outputs a string
\bm{z}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and a quantum state
\rho
.

5.

If
z_{j}=z_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{j}=1
then the experiment outputs
\rho
, and otherwise it outputs a special symbol
\bot
.

Note that the only difference between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
is that
\widetilde{r}_{k}
is set to be
r_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
. Let us assume
r_{k}=1
, since if
r_{k}
is
0
then
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
are identical. We construct
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
that distinguishes
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0)
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,1)
if
\mathpzc{A}
distinguishes between the hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\bm{z}}_{\bm{\theta}}):

1.

\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
plays the role of
\mathpzc{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
where the external challenger
\mathpzc{C}
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,b)
is used to simulate the challenger of
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
.
\mathpzc{C}
provides everything that should be sent to
\mathpzc{A}
(as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
).

2.

Suppose
\mathpzc{A}
sends a certificate
\mathsf{cert}=((\bm{z}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\bm{z}_{\ell}^{\prime}),\mathsf{cert}_{\mathsf{aDec}})
to the challenger where
\bm{z}_{i}^{\prime}=(z_{i,j}^{\prime})_{j\in[\lambda]}
for all
i\in[\ell]
. Then,
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
sets
\bm{z}^{\prime}=\bm{z}_{k}^{\prime}
.

3.

Outputs
\bm{z}^{\prime}
and the internal state
\rho
of
\mathpzc{A}
which it sends to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.

We observe that
\mathpzc{B}^{\prime}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k}
if
b=0
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1}
if
b=1
(since we are assuming
r_{k}=1
). Therefore, we can write

\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k})\leq\mathsf{TD}(\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,0),\widetilde{\mathsf{Expt}}_{\mathpzc{B}^{\prime},\mathpzc{C}}^{\mathsf{1,k}}(\lambda,1)).

(162)

Combining Equations
161
and
162
, we have

\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k-1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,k})\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(163)

Recall that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,0}\equiv\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1,\ell}\equiv\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. Therefore, combining the advantages of
\mathpzc{A}
in the sequence of intermediate hybrids as obtained in Equation
163
, we have

\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{0},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1})\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(164)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}:

This hybrid proceeds exactly similar to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except that the obfuscated circuit is now replaced with a simulated version of it. In particular,
\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CCO}.\mathpzc{Obf}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{aDec},R^{\ast},m_{0})

\widetilde{\mathpzc{aDec}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CCO}.\mathpzc{Sim}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{pp}_{\mathsf{aDec}},1^{|m_{b}|})

1.

The challenger computes

to

.

The adversary

\mathpzc{A}

and a pair of challenge messages

satisfying the fact that
P(x_{0})=P(x_{1})=0
for all
P
queried so far in the key query phase.

Sample

for all
i\in[\ell]

The experiment outputs
\rho
as a final output.

Since the information of lock string

is not used in generating the ABE ciphertexts

and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
are indistinguishable to
\mathpzc{A}
. In other words, we have

. If it does not hold, the challenger outputs

The experiment outputs
\rho
as a final output.

Since all the secret keys
\mathsf{sk}_{P}
corresponding to predicates
P
queried by the adversary satisy the condition that
P(x_{1})=0
, we can depend on the semantic security of ABE and show that the hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
are indistinguishable from
\mathpzc{A}
’s point of view using the similar argument that we used while establishing the indistinguishability between the hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. In other words, we have

\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{3},\mathsf{Hyb}_{4})\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(167)

Finally, we note that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
is the original certified everlasting experiment of
\Sigma_{\textsf{pe-ce}}
where the challenge bit is set to 1. Therefore, combing the advantages of
\mathpzc{A}
in the consecutive hybrids and applying the triangular inequality, we have

\mathsf{TD}(\mathsf{Hyb}_{0},\mathsf{Hyb}_{4})\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(168)

Finally, it is easy to show the computational indistinguishability, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\textsf{pe-ce}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma_{\textsf{pe-ce}},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(169)

We skip the formal description as it follows from the security of ABE and the security of
\mathsf{CECCO}
.
We can erase information about
R=(r_{,}\ldots,r_{\ell})
by the security of ABE. Then, we can apply the security of
\mathsf{CECCO}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Omitted Proofs for Collusion-Resistant FE

We prove the adaptive security of our collusion-resistant scheme
\mathsf{CED}
in
Section

3.3
.
That is, we show

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{CED},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,0)=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{C}\text{-}\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{CED},~\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,1)=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(170)

Let
\mathpzc{A}
be a QPT adversary against the adaptive security.
We consider the following sequence of hybrids.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the original adaptive security experiment where the challenge bit is set to be
0
. Specifically, it works as follows:

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK}
, and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
can make arbitrarily many key queries at any point of the experiment. When it makes a key query
f
, the challenger generates
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f])
and returns
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m^{(0)},m^{(1)})
to the challenger.
24
24

24

We use
(m^{(0)},m^{(1)})
instead of
(m_{0},m_{1})
to denote a pair of challenge messages to avoid a notational collision.
It must satisfy
f(m^{(0)})=f(m^{(1)})
for all key queries
f
that are made before or after sending
(m^{(0)},m^{(1)})
.

4.

The challenger generates
(\mathpzc{ct},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m^{(0)})
. Specifically,

(a)

Generate
z_{i},\theta_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
for every
i\in[2n+1]
.

(b)

Generate

and compute
v_{i,j,b}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j,b})
for every
i\in[2n+1]
,
j\in[\lambda]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
and set
U=(u_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
and
V\coloneqq(v_{i,j,b})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda],b\in\{0,1\}}
.

(c)

Generate a state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{i,j}}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\ket{z_{i,j}}\ket{u_{i,j,z_{i,j}}}&\text{~if~}\theta_{i,j}=0\\
\ket{0}\ket{u_{i,j,0}}+(-1)^{z_{i,j}}\ket{1}\ket{u_{i,j,1}}&\text{~if~}\theta_{i,j}=1\end{cases}

(171)

where
\theta_{i,j}
(resp.
z_{i,j}
) is the
j
-th bit of
\theta_{i}
(resp.
z_{i}
)
for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
.

(d)

Generate

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m_{i}^{(0)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n]\\
0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n+1,2n+1]\end{cases}.

(172)

(e)

Generate
\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{MPK},V\|\theta_{1}\|\ldots\|\theta_{2n+1}\|\beta_{1}\|\ldots\|\beta_{2n+1})
.

(f)

Set
\mathpzc{ct}=(\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{ct},\bigotimes_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}\ket{\psi_{i,j}})
and

.

If

Again, the challenger answers key queries from

.

7.

When
\mathpzc{A}
outputs a bit

if

holds for all key queries

.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This is identical to

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}

(173)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

This is identical to

for all

.

Note that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
is identical to the original adaptive security experiment where the challenge bit is set to be

. Thus, we only have to prove

\displaystyle|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(174)

We prove
Equation

174
by the following lemmata.

Lemma A.1.

If
\mathsf{PRG}
is a secure PRG,

\displaystyle|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(175)

Proof.

Noting that
u_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
for
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
is used only for generating
v_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
,
Lemma

A.1
directly follows from the security of
\mathsf{PRG}
.
Note that we can simulate
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{0},\mathsf{cert})
where
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i,j},d_{i,j})_{i,j}
since we need
\{z_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
and
\{u_{i,j,b}\}_{i,j,b}
such that
\theta_{i,j}=1
for verification.
∎

Lemma A.2.

If
\mathsf{FE}
is adaptively secure,

\displaystyle|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(176)

Proof.

For each
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
, there is no
u
such that
\mathsf{PRG}(u)=v_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
except for probability
2^{-\lambda}
. Let
\mathsf{Good}
be the event that the above holds for all
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
. We have
\Pr[\mathsf{Good}]\geq 1-(2n+1)\lambda 2^{-\lambda}=1-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
We prove that whenever
\mathsf{Good}
occurs, we have

\displaystyle g[f]((V,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2n+1},\beta_{1}^{(0)},\ldots,\beta_{2n+1}^{(0)}),(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]})

(177)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g[f]((V,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{2n+1},\beta_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\beta_{2n+1}^{(1)}),(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]})

(178)

for all key queries
f
and
(b_{i,j},u_{i,j})_{i\in[2n+1],j\in[\lambda]}

where

\displaystyle\beta_{i}^{(a)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m_{i}^{(a)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n]\\
0\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}z_{i,j}&\text{if~}i\in[n+1,2n+1]\end{cases}

(179)

for
a\in\{0,1\}
.
If this is proven,
Lemma

A.2
directly follows from the adaptive security of
\mathsf{FE}
.

Below, we prove
Equation

177
.
We consider the following two cases.

•

If
\mathsf{PRG}(u_{i,j})=v_{i,j,b_{i,j}}
holds for every
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
, then by the assumption that
\mathsf{Good}
occurs, we have
b_{i,j}=z_{i,j}
for all
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
. Then we have
\beta_{i}^{(a)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{i,j}=0}b_{i,j}=m^{(a)}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
and
\beta_{2n+1}^{(a)}\oplus\bigoplus_{j:\theta_{2n+1,j}=0}b_{2n+1,j}=0
for
a\in\{0,1\}
. Then the LHS of
Equation

177
is equal to
f(m^{(0)})
and the RHS of
Equation

177
is equal to
f(m^{(1)})
. By the restriction on
\mathpzc{A}
in the adaptive security experiment, we have
f(m^{(0)})=f(m^{(1)})
. Therefore, both sides of
Equation

177
are equal to
f(m^{(0)})=f(m^{(1)})
.

•

Otherwise, both sides of
Equation

177
are equal to
\bot
.

In either case,
Equation

177
holds.
Note that we can simulate
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{b},\mathsf{cert})
where
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i,j},d_{i,j})_{i,j}
since we need
\{z_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
and
\{u_{i,j,b}\}_{i,j,b}
such that
\theta_{i,j}=1
for verification.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

A.2
.
∎

Lemma A.3.

If
\mathsf{PRG}
is a secure PRG,

\displaystyle|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(180)

Proof.

Noting that
u_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
for
i\in[2n+1]
and
j\in[\lambda]
such that
\theta_{i,j}=0
is used only for generating
v_{i,j,1\oplus z_{i,j}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
,
Lemma

A.1
directly follows from the security of
\mathsf{PRG}
.
Note that we can simulate
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}_{1},\mathsf{cert})
where
\mathsf{cert}=(c_{i,j},d_{i,j})_{i,j}
since we need
\{z_{i,j}\}_{i,j}
and
\{u_{i,j,b}\}_{i,j,b}
such that
\theta_{i,j}=1
for verification.
∎

Appendix B
Adaptively Secure Public-Slot PKFE

In this section, we present an adaptively secure public-slot PKFE scheme based on

•

Selectively secure PKFE,

•

Selectively single-key function private SKFE, and

•

Adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE.

We need to show how to achieve adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE since it is an essentail building block.
Our adaptively secure public-slot PKFE scheme is presented in
Section

B.4
.

We present an adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE scheme based on

•

Selectively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE and

•

Receiver non-committing encryption

in
Section

B.3
.

We also present a selectively secure single-ciphertext SKFE with public scheme based on IO and OWFs. This construction uses an MIFE scheme whose arity is
2
(i.e.,
2
-input FE) by Goldwasser et al.
[
GGG+14
]
. We introduce necessary tools and definitions in
Sections

B.1
and
B.2
.

B.1
Building Blocks

We introduce building blocks for our adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE scheme.

Non-committing encryption.

We recall the notion of (secret-key) receiver non-committing encryption (NCE)
[
CFGN96
,
JL00
,
CHK05
]
.

Definition B.1 (Secret-Key RNCE (Syntax)).

A secret-key NCE scheme is a tuple of PPT algorithms
(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\allowbreak\mathsf{Fake},\mathsf{Reveal})
with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{aux})
:

The key generation algorithm takes as input the security
parameter
1^{\lambda}
and outputs a key pair
(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{dk})
and an auxiliary information
\mathsf{aux}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m)\rightarrow\mathsf{ct}
:

The encryption algorithm takes as input
\mathsf{ek}
and a plaintext
m\in\mathcal{M}
and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow m^{\prime}\mbox{ or }\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes as input
\mathsf{dk}
and
\mathsf{ct}
and outputs a plaintext
m^{\prime}
or
\bot
.

\mathsf{Fake}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{aux})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}
:

The fake encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{dk}
and
\mathsf{aux}
, and outputs a fake ciphertext
\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}
.

\mathsf{Reveal}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{aux},\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}},m)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}
:

The reveal algorithm takes
\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{aux},\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}
and
m
, and outputs a fake secret key
\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}
.

Definition B.2 (Correctness of secret-key NCE).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[m^{\prime}\neq m\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m)\\
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition B.3 (Receiver Non-Committing (RNC) Security for SKE).

A secret-key NCE scheme is RNC secure if it satisfies the following.
Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Fake},\mathsf{Reveal})
be a secret-key NCE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sk}\mbox{-}\mathsf{rec}}{nc}(\lambda,b)
.

1.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
1^{\lambda}
to the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends an encryption query
m
to the challenger. The challenger computes and returns
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m)
to
\mathpzc{A}
. This process can be repeated polynomially many times.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends a query
m\in\mathcal{M}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger does the following.

•

If
b=0
, the challenger generates
\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ek},m)
and returns
(\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{dk})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

If
b=1
, the challenger generates
\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Fake}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{aux})
and

\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Reveal}(\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{aux},\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}},m)
and returns
(\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}},\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

Again
\mathpzc{A}
can send encryption queries.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

Let
\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sk}\mbox{-}\mathsf{rec}\mbox{-}\mathsf{nc}}(\lambda)
be the advantage of the experiment above.
We say that the
\Sigma
is RNC secure if for any QPT adversary, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sk}\mbox{-}\mathsf{rec}\mbox{-}\mathsf{nc}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[ \expc{\Sigma,\qA}{sk}{rec}{nc}(\secp, 0)=1] - \Pr[ \expc{\Sigma,\qA}{sk}{rec}{nc}(\secp, 1)=1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(184)

Theorem B.4 (
[
KNTY19
, Section 7.2 in the eprint version]
).

If there exists an IND-CPA secure SKE scheme (against QPT adversaries), there exists an RNC secure secret-key NCE scheme (against QPT adversaries) with plaintext space
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, where
\ell
is some polynomial, respectively.

Functional Encryption.

Definition B.5 (Public-Key FE (Syntax)).

A public-key functional encryption (PKFE) scheme for a class
\mathcal{F}
of functions is a tuple of PPT algorithms

\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}
, master public key space
\mathcal{MPK}
, master secret key space
\mathcal{MSK}
, and secret key space
\mathcal{SK}
,
that work as follows.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})
:

The setup algorithm takes the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input, and outputs a master public key
\mathsf{MPK}\in\mathcal{MPK}

and a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}\in\mathcal{MSK}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}_{f}
:

The key generation algorithm takes
\mathsf{MSK}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
as input,
and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\rightarrow\mathsf{CT}
:

The encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{MPK}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
as input,
and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT})\rightarrow y

\mbox{\bf or }\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and
\mathsf{CT}
as input, and outputs
y
or
\bot
.

We require that a PKFE scheme satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition B.6 (Correctness of PKFE).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\Pr\left[y\neq f(m)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\\
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)\\
y\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition B.7 (Selective Security of PKFE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a PKFE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, computes
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m_{b})
, and sends
(\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{CT})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to make arbitrarily many key queries.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.
If
f_{\ell}(m_{0})=f_{\ell}(m_{1})
holds for all key queries
f_{\ell}
, the experiment outputs
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is adaptively secure if for any
QPT
adversary
\mathpzc{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{sel}{ind}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{sel}{ind}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(189)

Definition B.8 (Secret-Key FE (Syntax)).

A secret-key functional encryption (SKFE) scheme for a class
\mathcal{F}
of functions is a tuple of PPT algorithms

\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}
, master secret key space
\mathcal{MSK}
, and secret key space
\mathcal{SK}
,
that work as follows.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathsf{MSK}
:

The setup algorithm takes the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input, and outputs a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}\in\mathcal{MSK}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}_{f}
:

The key generation algorithm takes
\mathsf{MSK}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
as input,
and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MSK},m)\rightarrow\mathsf{CT}
:

The encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{MSK}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
as input,
and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT})\rightarrow y

\mbox{\bf or }\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and
\mathsf{CT}
as input, and outputs
y
or
\bot
.

We require that an SKFE scheme satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition B.9 (Correctness of SKFE).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathcal{M}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\Pr\left[y\neq f(m)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{MSK}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\\
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MSK},m)\\
y\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition B.10 (Adaptive Single-Key Single-Ciphertext Security of SKFE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be an SKFE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{MSK}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

The adversary makes the following encryption query and key query in no particular order.

•

\mathpzc{A}
sends
f\in\mathcal{F}
to the challenger. The challenger computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)
, and returns
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
\mathpzc{A}
can do this process one once.

•

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger. The challenger computes
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MSK},m_{b})
, and returns
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
\mathpzc{A}
can do this process one once.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.
If
f(m_{0})=f(m_{1})
holds, the experiment outputs
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is adaptively single-key single-ciphertext secure if for any
QPT
adversary
\mathpzc{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expc{\Sigma,\qA}{ada}{1key}{1ct}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expc{\Sigma,\qA}{ada}{1key}{1ct}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(194)

Definition B.11 (Selective Single-Key Function Privacy of SKFE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be an SKFE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{fp}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The challenger sends
(m_{1,0},m_{1,1}),\ldots,(m_{q,0},m_{q,1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2q}
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{MSK}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, computes
\mathsf{CT}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MSK},m_{i,b})
for all
i\in[q]
, and returns
(\mathsf{CT}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{CT}_{q})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(f_{0},f_{1})\in\mathcal{F}^{2}
to the challenger. The challenger computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{b}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{b})
, and returns
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{b}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
\mathpzc{A}
can do this process only once.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.
If
f_{0}(m_{i,0})=f_{1}(m_{i,1})
holds for all
i\in[q]
, the experiment outputs
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is selectively single-key function private if for any
QPT
adversary
\mathpzc{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{fp}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expc{\Sigma,\qA}{sel}{1key}{fp}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expc{\Sigma,\qA}{sel}{1key}{fp}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(195)

2-input FE.

We recall the notion of
2
-input FE. The following definitions are special cases of multi-input functional encryption (MIFE) by Goldwasser et al.
[
GGG+14
]
.

Definition B.12 (
2
-input FE (Syntax)).

A
2
-input FE scheme for a class
\mathcal{F}
of functions is a tuple of PPT algorithms

\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}
, master secret key space
\mathcal{MSK}
, and secret key space
\mathcal{SK}
,
that work as follows.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathsf{MSK}
:

The setup algorithm takes the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input, and outputs a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}\in\mathcal{MSK}
and two encryption keys
\mathsf{EK}_{1}
and
\mathsf{EK}_{2}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}_{f}
:

The key generation algorithm takes
\mathsf{MSK}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
as input,
and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{EK}_{i},x)\rightarrow\mathsf{CT}_{i}
:

The encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{EK}_{i}
and
x\in\mathcal{M}
