as input,
and outputs a ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2})\rightarrow z

\mbox{\bf or }\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and
(\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2})
as input, and outputs
y
or
\bot
.

We require that a
2
-input FE scheme satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition B.13 (Correctness of
2
-input FE).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,
(x,y)\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\Pr\left[z\neq f(x,y)\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{MSK},\mathsf{EK}_{1},\mathsf{EK}_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)\\
\mathsf{CT}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{EK}_{1},x),\mathsf{CT}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{EK}_{2},y)\\
z\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition B.14 (
(1,1)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
Security of
2
-FE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a
2
-input FE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The adversary sends
((x_{0},y_{0}),(x_{1},y_{1}))
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{MSK},\mathsf{EK}_{1},\mathsf{EK}_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, computes
\mathsf{CT}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{EK}_{1},x_{b})
and
\mathsf{CT}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{EK}_{2},y_{b})
, and sends
(\mathsf{EK}_{2},\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
can send a key query
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
to the challenger. The challenger computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{i})
, and returns
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
\mathpzc{A}
can send unbounded polynomially many key queries. Let
q_{k}
be the total number of the key queries.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.
If
f_{i}(x_{0},y)=f_{i}(x_{1},y)
holds for all
y\in\mathcal{M}
and
i\in[q_{k}]
(we call
\mathpzc{A}
is valid), the experiment outputs
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is
(1,1)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
secure if for any
QPT
adversary
\mathpzc{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{sel}{1ct}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{sel}{1ct}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(200)

Here,
(1,1)
means that
\mathpzc{A}
is given
one
encryption key
\mathsf{EK}_{2}
and
one
challenge ciphertext vector
(\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2})
.

The security definition above is a special case of
(t,q)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
security by Goldwasser et al.
[
GGG+14
]
, where
t
is the number of corrupted encryption keys and
q
is the number of challenge ciphertext vectors.

Theorem B.15 (
[
GGG+14
]
).

If there exist IO and OWFs, there exists
(1,1)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
secure
2
-input FE for all polynomial-size circuits.

Although Goldwasser et al. proved a more general theorem (
(t,q)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
secure
n
-input FE where
t\leq n
and
q
is an a-priori bounded polynomial), the simplified version above is sufficient for our purpose.

B.2
Variants of Security Definitions

We can consider the secret-key variant of
Definition

3.9
, where
\mathsf{Setup}
generates only a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}
and
\mathsf{Enc}
uses
\mathsf{MSK}
instead of
\mathsf{MPK}
. Correctness of public-slot SKFE is a natural extension of
Definition

3.10
.
We omit syntax and correctness for public-slot SKFE.

Below, we introduce variants of security definitions for public-slot SKFE.

Definition B.16 (Single-Key Security of Public-Slot SKFE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a public-slot SKFE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{MSK}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to make arbitrarily many key queries.
For the
\ell
-th key query, the challenger receives
f_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
,
computes
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f_{\ell})
,
and sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{\ell}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
can send a challenge plaintext pair
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MSK},m_{b})
and sends
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

Again,
\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to make arbitrarily many key queries.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.
If
f_{\ell}(m_{0},\mathsf{pub})=f_{\ell}(m_{1},\mathsf{pub})
holds for all key queries
f_{\ell}
and public inputs
\mathsf{pub}\in\mathcal{P}
, the experiment outputs
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is adaptively single-ciphertext secure if for any
QPT
adversary
\mathpzc{A}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ada}{1ct}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ada}{1ct}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(201)

If the adversary must declare the challenge plaintext pair
(m_{0},m_{1})
at the very beginning of the experiment, we say that
\Sigma
is selectively single-ciphertext secure and denote the advantage and experiment by
\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda)
and
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda)
, respectively.

If the adversary is allowed to make only one key query, we say
\Sigma
is adaptively/selectively single-key single-ciphertext secure and denote the advantage and experiment by
\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{xxx}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda)
and
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{xx}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda)
, respectively, where
\mathsf{xxx}\in\{\mathsf{sel},\mathsf{ada}\}
.

B.3
Adaptively Single-Key Single-Ciphertext Public-Slot SKFE Scheme

Selectively single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE.

First, we present our selectively single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE scheme
\mathsf{1selFE}
.

Ingredients.

•

(1,1)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
secure
2
-input FE
\mathsf{2FE}=\mathsf{2FE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
for all polynomial-size circuits.

Scheme description.

Our scheme
\mathsf{1selFE}=\mathsf{1selFE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is as follows.

\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Output
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2})
.

\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2})
.

2.

Generate
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{SK}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},f)
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{SK}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}
.

\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MSK},m)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2})
.

2.

Generate
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{1},m)
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2})
.

\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{SK}_{f},\mathsf{CT},\mathsf{pub})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{SK}_{f}=\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}
and
\mathsf{CT}=(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2})
.

2.

Compute
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2},\mathsf{pub})
.

3.

Compute and output
y\coloneqq\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{2})
.

Correctness.

It is easy to see correctness holds due to correctness of
\mathsf{2FE}
.

Theorem B.17.

If
\mathsf{2FE}
is
(1,1)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
secure
2
-input FE for all polynomial-size circuits,
\mathsf{1selFE}
is selectively single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE for all polynomial-size circuits.

This theorem immediately yields a selectively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE for all polynomial-size circuits.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1selFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,b)
denote the selective single-ciphertext security of public-slot SKFE.
We construct an algorithm
\mathpzc{B}
that breaks
(1,1)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
security of
\mathsf{2FE}
by using an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
that breaks selectively single-ciphertext security of
\mathsf{1selFE}
.
\mathpzc{B}
does the following.

1.

First,
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})
. Then,
\mathpzc{B}
chooses a random
y\leftarrow\mathcal{M}
, sets
(x_{0},x_{1})\coloneqq(m_{0},m_{1})
and
(y_{0},y_{1})\coloneqq(y,y)
, and passes
((x_{0},y_{0}),(x_{1},y_{1}))
to its challenger.

2.

When
\mathpzc{B}
receives
(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{2})
from its challenger,
\mathpzc{B}
sets
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{1},\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2})
and passes
\mathsf{CT}
to
\mathpzc{A}

3.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends a key query
f_{i}
,
\mathpzc{B}
passes
f_{i}
to its challenger, receives
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},f_{i})
, and passes
\mathsf{SK}_{f_{i}}\coloneqq\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}
,
\mathpzc{B}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.

If
\mathpzc{A}
is valid in the experiment of selective single-ciphertext security for public-slot SKFE
\mathsf{1selFE}
, it holds
f_{i}(x_{0},y^{\prime})=f_{i}(x_{1},y^{\prime})
for all
i\in[q]
and
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
. Then,
\mathpzc{B}
is also a valid adversary in the experiment of
(1,1)
-
\mathsf{sel}
-
\mathsf{ind}
security for
\mathsf{2FE}
since
\mathpzc{B}
received
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ek}_{2}
.
It is easy to see the following.

•

If
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},x_{0})
and
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},y)
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1selFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,0)
.

•

If
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},x_{1})
and
\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{ct}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{2FE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{2fe}.\mathsf{msk},y)
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1selFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,1)
.

Thus, if
\mathpzc{A}
distinguishes
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1selFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,0)
from
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1selFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,1)
,
\mathpzc{B}
distinguishes
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{2FE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,0)
from
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{2FE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,1)
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE.

Next, we present our adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE scheme
\mathsf{1adaFE}
.

Ingredients.

•

Selectively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE
\mathsf{1selFE}=\mathsf{1selFE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
for all polynomial-size circuits.

•

Receiver non-committing encryption
\mathsf{NCE}=\mathsf{NCE}.(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Fake},\mathsf{Reveal})
.

Scheme description.

Our scheme
\mathsf{1adaFE}=\mathsf{1adaFE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is as follows.

\mathsf{1adaFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

1.

Generate
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Generate
(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})
.

\mathsf{1adaFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{MSK},f)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{MSK},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})
.

2.

Generate
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},f)
.

3.

Generate
\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{fe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f})
.

4.

Output
\mathsf{SK}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathsf{1adaFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MSK},m)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})
.

2.

Generate
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},m)
.

3.

Output
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk})
.

\mathsf{1adaFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{SK}_{f},\mathsf{CT},\mathsf{pub})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{SK}_{f}=\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}
and
\mathsf{CT}=(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk})
.

2.

Compute
\mathsf{sk}_{f}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct})
.

3.

Compute and output
y\coloneqq\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f}^{\prime},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{pub})
.

Correctness.

It is easy to see correctness holds due to correctness of
\mathsf{1selFE}
and
\mathsf{NCE}
.

Theorem B.18.

If
\mathsf{1selFE}
is selectively single-key single-ciphertext secure public-slot SKFE for all polynomial-size circuits and
\mathsf{NCE}
is RNC secure,
\mathsf{1adaFE}
is adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE for all polynomial-size circuits.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}(b)
denote
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1adaFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,b)
.
We define a hybrid game
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}(b)
as follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}(b)
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1adaFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,b)
except that:

1.

if
\mathpzc{A}
sends a key query
f
before an encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
, we generate
\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Fake}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})
instead of
\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f})
and return
\mathsf{SK}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}
for the key query.

2.

when
\mathpzc{A}
sends an encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
after the key query
f
above, we generate
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},m_{b})
,
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},f)
, and
\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Reveal}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\allowbreak\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux},\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f})
, and return
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}})
for the encryption query.

First, we show the following.

Proposition B.19.

It holds
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hyb_0(b)=1] -\Pr[\hyb_1(b)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
if
\mathsf{NCE}
is RNC secure.

We construct an algorithm
\mathpzc{B}
that breaks RNC security of
\mathsf{NCE}
by using an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
that breaks adaptive single-key single-ciphertext security of
\mathsf{1adaFE}
. Note that if
\mathpzc{A}
sends an encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
before a key query
f
, these two games are the same. We focus on the case where
\mathpzc{A}
sends a key query
f
before an encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
.
\mathpzc{B}
does the following.

1.

First,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends a key query
f
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},f)
, sends
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}
to its challenger, and receives
(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk}^{\ast})
.
\mathpzc{B}
passes
\mathsf{SK}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

After the key query
f
above, for an encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},m_{b})
and returns
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk}^{\ast})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}
,
\mathpzc{B}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.

It is easy to see the following.

•

If
\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f})
and
\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk}^{\ast}=\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk}
where
(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek}.\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}(b)
.

•

If
\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\coloneqq\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Fake}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})
and
\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Reveal}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux},\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f})
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}(b)
.

Thus, if
\mathpzc{A}
distinguishes
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}(b)
from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}(b)
,
\mathpzc{B}
distinguishes
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{NCE}.\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{sk}\mbox{-}\mathsf{rec}\mbox{-}\mathsf{nc}}(\lambda,b)
.

Next, we show the following.

Proposition B.20.

It holds
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hyb_1(0)=1] -\Pr[\hyb_1(1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
if
\mathsf{1selFE}
is selectively single-key single-ciphertext secure.

We construct an algorithm
\mathpzc{B}
that breaks selective single-key single-ciphertext security of
\mathsf{1selFE}
by using an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
that breaks adaptive single-key single-ciphertext security of
\mathsf{1adaFE}
.

\mathpzc{B}
does the following.

1.

First,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

There are the following two cases:

•

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends a key query
f
before an encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Fake}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\allowbreak\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{aux})
passes
\mathsf{SK}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
. After the key query
f
above, for an encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
,
\mathpzc{B}
passes
(m_{0},m_{1})
to its challenger and receives
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
. Then,
\mathpzc{B}
sends
f
to its challenger and receives
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},f)
. Finally,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Reveal}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk},\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{ct}},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f})
and sends
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{nce}.\widetilde{\mathsf{dk}})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

•

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends an encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
before a key query
f
,
\mathpzc{B}
passes
(m_{0},m_{1})
to its challenger, receives
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}
, and returns
\mathsf{CT}\coloneqq(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast},\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{dk})
to
\mathpzc{A}
. After the encryption query
(m_{0},m_{1})
above, for a key query
f
,
\mathpzc{B}
passes
f
to its challenger and receives
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},f)
.
\mathpzc{B}
returns
\mathsf{SK}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{NCE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{nce}.\mathsf{ek},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{sk}_{f})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}
,
\mathpzc{B}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.

Note that
\mathpzc{B}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})
to its challenger before it sends
f
as a key query in the both cases above.
If
\mathpzc{A}
is a valid adversary in the experiment of adaptive single-key single-ciphertext security for public-lot SKFE
\mathsf{1adaFE}
, it holds
f(m_{0},y^{\prime})=f(m_{1},y^{\prime})
for all
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
.
Then,
\mathpzc{B}
is also a valid adversary in the experiment of selective single-key single-ciphertext security for public-slot SKFE
\mathsf{1selFE}
.
In addition, it is easy to see the following.

•

If
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},m_{0})
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}(0)
.

•

If
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathsf{1selFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},m_{1})
,
\mathpzc{B}
perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}(1)
.

Thus, if
\mathpzc{A}
distinguishes
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}(0)
from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}(1)
,
\mathpzc{B}
distinguishes
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1selFE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,0)
from
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{1selFE},\mathpzc{B}}^{\mathsf{sel}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1key}\mbox{-}\mathsf{1ct}}(\lambda,1)
.

Therefore, we obtain
\absolutevalue{\Pr[\hyb_0(0)=1]-\Pr[\hyb_0(1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
, which is our goal.
∎

B.4
Adaptively Secure Public-Slot PKFE Scheme

Note that the construction in this section is bassically the same as that by Ananth and Sahai
[
AS16
]
except that we use single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE as a building block istead of single-key single-ciphertext standard SKFE.

Ingredients.

•

Selectively secure PKFE
\mathsf{PKFE}=\mathsf{PKFE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
for all polynonial-size circuits.

•

Selectively single-key function private SKFE
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}=\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
for polynomial-size circuits.

•

Adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE
\mathsf{SKFE}=\mathsf{SKFE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
for polynomial-size circuits.

•

A PRF
\mathsf{PRF}:\mathcal{K}\times\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

•

SKE with pseudorandom ciphertext
\mathsf{SKE}=\mathsf{SKE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
.

Scheme description.

The adaptively secure public-slot PKFE scheme
\mathsf{FE}=\mathsf{FE}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is as follows.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Output
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{msk},f)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{MSK}=\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK}
.

2.

Sample
C_{\mathsf{ske}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{\mathsf{ske}}(\lambda)}
where
\ell_{\mathsf{ske}}(\lambda)
is the length of a SKE ciphertext that encrypts a string of length
\ell_{\mathsf{skfe}}(\lambda)+\ell_{\mathsf{1keyskfe}}(\lambda)
. We denote
\ell_{\mathsf{skfe}}(\lambda)
by the length of a SKFE secret key and
\ell_{\mathsf{1keyskfe}}(\lambda)
by the length of a
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
ciphertext.

3.

Sample
\tau\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{4\lambda}

4.

Generate
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f,C_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau]}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f,C_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau])
where
g[f,C_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau]
is a function described in
Figure

4
.

5.

Output
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f,C_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau]}
.

g[f,C_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau]

Input:

\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK},\textsf{K},\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK},\beta

1.

Parse
\tau=(\tau_{0}\|\tau_{1}\|\tau_{2}\|\tau_{3})
.

2.

If
\beta=0
then

•

Compute
R_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}(\textsf{K},\tau_{i})
for
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

•

Generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};R_{0})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK},f;R_{1})
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK},R_{2},0);R_{3})
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

3.

Else,

•

Compute
(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct})\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK},C_{\mathsf{ske}})
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

Figure 4:
The description of the function
g[f,C_{E},\tau]

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{MPK},m)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{MPK}=\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK}
.

2.

Sample
\textsf{K}\leftarrow\mathcal{K}
.

3.

Generate
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

4.

Generate
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m]}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK},h[m])
where
h[m]
is a function described in
Figure

5
.

5.

Compute
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK},\textsf{K},\bot,0))
.

6.

Output
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m]},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

h[m]

Input:

\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK},R,\alpha

1.

If
\alpha=0
then

•

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK},m;R)
.

•

Output
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}
.

2.

Else, output
\bot
.

Figure 5:
The description of the function
h[m]

\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{ct},y)
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}
and
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m]},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

2.

Compute
(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

3.

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m]},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct})
.

4.

Compute
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct},y)

5.

Output
m^{\prime}
.

The security proof is almost the same as that of Ananth and Sahai
[
AS16
]
. We provide the proof for confirmation since we use adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE.

Theorem B.21.

If
\mathsf{PKFE}
is selectively secure PKFE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
,
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
is selectively single-key function private SKFE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
,
\mathsf{SKFE}
is adaptively single-key single-ciphertext public-slot SKFE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
,
\mathsf{PRF}
is a secure PRF, and
\mathsf{SKE}
is ciphertext pseudorandom,
FE
is adaptively indistinguishable-secure public-slot PKFE for
\mathsf{P/poly}
.

We immediately obtain
Theorem

3.12
from the thereom above.

Correctness.

Let
\mathsf{ct}=(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m]},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct})
be an honestly generated ciphertext encrypting a message
m
and
\mathsf{sk}_{f}=\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]}
be an honestly generated secret key corresponding to a function
f
. Firstly, we note that
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}
is an encryption of the message
(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK},\textsf{K},\bot,0)
and
g[f]
is a function that takes
(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK},\textsf{K},\bot,0)
as input and outputs a SKFE secret key
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}
corresponding to the function
f
and a single key SKFE ciphertext
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}
. Therefore, by the correctness of PKFE, the decryption algorithm
\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f]},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct})
yields
g[f](\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK},\textsf{K},\bot,0)=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct})
. Secondly, we observe that
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}
encrypts a message
(\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{MSK},R_{2},0)
and
h[m]
is a function that takes
(\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{MSK},R_{2},0)
an input and outputs a SKFE ciphertext
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}
. Therefore, by the correctness of SKFE, the decryption algorithm
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m]},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct})
yields
h[m](\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{MSK},R_{2},0)=\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}
. Finally, we note that
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}
encrypts the message
m
and
f
is a function that takes
(m,y)
as input and outputs
f(m,y)
where
y
is an input to the public slot. Thus, by the correctness of public-slot SKFE, we obtain
\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct},y)=m^{\prime}=f(m,y)
.

Adaptive Security.

We prove
Theorem

B.21
.

Proof of
Theorem

B.21
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a PPT adversary against the adaptive security of the public-slot PKFE. We use the following sequence of hybrids to prove the security. Let
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{i}=1]
be the winning probability of
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{i}
for all
i
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

This is the original adaptive security experiment where the challenge bit set to 0. Specifically, it works as follows:

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK}
, and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

The challenger samples a PRF key
\textsf{K}^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{K}
and generate a master secret key
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},\textsf{K}^{*},\bot,0))
.

4.

\mathcal{A}
can make arbitrarily many key queries at any point of the experiment. When it makes the
j
-th key query for a function
f_{j}
, the challenger works as follows:

(a)

Sample
C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}=(\tau_{j,0}\|\tau_{j,1}\|\tau_{j,2}\|\tau_{j,3})
uniformly at random.

(b)

Generate
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}])
where
g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]
is a function described in
Figure

4
.

(c)

Set
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}
.

The challenger sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

5.

\mathcal{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger. It must satisfy
f(m_{0},y)=f(m_{1},y)
for any public input
y
and for all key queries
f
that are made before or after sending
(m_{0},m_{1})
.

6.

The challenger computes the ciphertext as follows:

(a)

Generate
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0}]}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},h[m_{0}])
where
h[m_{0}]
is a function described in
Figure

5
.

(b)

Set
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0}]}^{*},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*})
where
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}
is computed in Step 3.

The challenger sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

7.

\mathcal{A}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
which is the final output of the experiment.

Note that, the challenger can sample a PRF key
K^{*}
, a master secret key for the single key function-private SKFE
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}
before it answers any secret key query. Moreover, the challenger can also compute the part of the challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}
before the key query phase.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except the challenger samples a SKE key
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*}
before it answers any key query and sets
C_{j,\mathsf{ske}}
to be the ciphertext of SKE which corresponds to the challenge ciphertext. More specifically, the challenger answers to the
j
-th key query for a function
f_{j}
as follows:

(a)

Sample
\tau_{j}=(\tau_{j,0}\|\tau_{j,1}\|\tau_{j,2}\|\tau_{j,3})
uniformly at random.

(b)

Compute
R_{j,i}=\mathsf{PRF}(\textsf{K}^{*},\tau_{j,i})
for all
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

(c)

Generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};R_{j,0})
.

(d)

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},f_{j};R_{j,1})
.

(e)

Compute
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0);R_{j,3})
.

(f)

Compute
C_{j,\mathsf{ske}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},u_{j})
where
u_{j}=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j})
.

(g)

Generate
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}])
where
g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]
is a function described in
Figure

4
.

(h)

Set
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}
.

The challenger sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}}
to
\mathcal{A}
. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{SKE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of SKE. In particular, consider
\mathcal{B}_{1}
to be an adversary against the security of SKE. When
\mathcal{A}
queries a secret key for a function
f_{j}
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
proceeds as in Step (a) to (f) and sends the message
u_{j}=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j})
to it’s challenger. Upon receiving a ciphertext
C_{j,\mathsf{ske}}
from the challenger,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
computes
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}
and sends it to
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{B}_{1}
receives a random string then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
, otherwise, if
\mathcal{B}_{1}
is sent an encryption of
u_{j}
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
. Therefore, the wining probability of
\mathcal{B}_{1}
is the same as
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}
. Hence, by the security of SKE, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(202)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
except the challenger computes

\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},(\bot,\bot,\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},1))

instead of computing
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},\textsf{K}^{*},\bot,0))
. In particular, the mode of decryption is changed to
\beta=1
from
\beta=0
meaning that
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*}
is used to decrypt
C_{j,\mathsf{ske}}
to get an output of
(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j})
while decryption of
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
is performed by the
j
-th secret key
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}
. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{PKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of PKFE. Let us consider an adversary
\mathcal{B}_{2}
against the security of PKFE. Firstly,
\mathcal{B}_{2}
sends a pair of challenge message
((\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},\textsf{K}^{*},\bot,0),(\bot,\bot,\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},1))
to it’s challenger and receives the public key
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK}
and a ciphertext
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}
. Note that
\mathcal{B}_{2}
can choose the challenge message independent of all the queries of
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, a selectively secure PKFE is sufficient for arguing the indistinguishability between the hybrids. Whenever
\mathcal{B}_{2}
receives a secret key query from
\mathcal{A}
for a function
f_{j}
, it queries for a secret key to it’s challenger for a function
g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]
and returns the output to
\mathcal{A}
. Firstly,
\mathcal{B}_{2}
is an admissible adversary as

g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}](\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},\textsf{K}^{*},\bot,0)=g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}](\bot,\bot,\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},1)

holds for all
j
. If
\mathcal{B}_{2}
receives an encryption of
(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},\textsf{K}^{*},\bot,0)
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
, otherwise, if
\mathcal{B}_{2}
receives an encryption of
(\bot,\bot,\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},1)
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
. Therefore, the winning probability of
\mathcal{B}_{2}
is essentially the same as
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}
. Hence, by the security of PKFE, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(203)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
except the challenger samples
R_{j,i}
uniformly at random for all
j,i
, while answering the secret key queries instead of computing these values using the PRF key
\textsf{K}^{*}
. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{PRF}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\textsf{K}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of PRF. In other words, if
\mathcal{B}_{3}
is an adversary against the security of PRF then the winning probability of
\mathcal{B}_{3}
is the same as
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}
. Hence, by the security of PRF, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(204)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
except the challenger generates

\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0},m_{1},v]}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},h[m_{0},m_{1},v])

and sets the challenge ciphertext as
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0},m_{1},v]}^{*},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*})
where
h[m_{0},m_{1},v]
is a function described in
Figure

6
and
v
is a random string. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
. Let us consider an adversary
\mathcal{B}_{4}
against the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
. We assume that
Q
be the total number of secret key queries the adversary
\mathcal{A}
makes in the experiment. At first,
\mathcal{B}_{4}
prepares a list of
Q
challenge messages
(M_{1},\ldots,M_{Q})
where
M_{j}=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0)
for
j\in[Q]
. More precisely,
\mathcal{B}
sends
(M_{j},M_{j})
for all
j\in[Q]
and receives the ciphertexts as
\{\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j}\}_{j\in[Q]}
which is used in answering
\mathcal{A}
’s secret key queries as in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
or
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
. When
\mathcal{A}
sends the challenge message tuple
(m_{0},m_{1})
,
\mathcal{B}_{4}
queries for a secret key with the pair of functions
(h[m_{0}],h[m_{0},m_{1},v])
to it’s challenger. Then,
\mathcal{B}_{4}
uses the output from it’s challenger to create the challenge ciphertext for
\mathcal{A}
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{B}_{4}
is an admissible adversary for the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since

h[m_{0}](\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0)=h[m_{0},m_{1},v](\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0)

holds for all
j\in[Q]
. If
\mathcal{B}_{4}
receives a secret key
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0}]}^{*}
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
, otherwise, if
\mathcal{B}_{4}
receives a secret key
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0},m_{1},v]}^{*}
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
. Therefore, the winning probability of
\mathcal{B}_{4}
is essentially the same as
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}
. Hence, by the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(205)

h[m,m^{\prime},v]

Input:

\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK},R,\alpha

1.

If
\alpha=0
then

•

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK},m;R)
.

•

Output
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}
.

2.

If
\alpha=1
then

•

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK},m^{\prime};R)
.

•

Output
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}
.

3.

Else, output
v
.

Figure 6:
The description of the function
h[m,m^{\prime},v]

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
except the challenger computes

\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},1);R_{j,3})

while generating the
j
-th secret key corresponding to a function
f_{j}
for all
j
. In Lemma
B.22
, we show that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(206)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
except the challenger generates

\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{1},m_{1},v]}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},h[m_{1},m_{1},v])

and sets the challenge ciphertext as
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{1},m_{1},v]}^{*},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*})
where
h[m_{1},m_{1},v]
is a function as described in
Figure

6
. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
. The simulation strategy is similar to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
. By the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(207)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
except the challenger computes

\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0);R_{j,3})

while generating the
j
-th secret key corresponding to a function
f_{j}
for all
j
. Moreover, the challenger generates

\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{1}]}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},h[m_{1}])

and sets the challenge ciphertext as
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{1}]}^{*},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*})
where
h[m_{1}]
is a function as described in
Figure

5
. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
. Let us consider an adversary
\mathcal{B}_{7}
against the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
.

At first,
\mathcal{B}_{7}
prepares a list of
Q
challenge message pairs
((M_{1}^{(0)},M_{1}^{(1)}),\ldots,(M_{Q}^{(0)},M_{Q}^{(1)}))
where

M_{j}^{(0)}=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},1)~~\text{ and }~~M_{j}^{(1)}=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0)

for
j\in[Q]
. More precisely,
\mathcal{B}_{7}
sends
(M_{j}^{(0)},M_{j}^{(1)})
for all
j\in[Q]
and receives the ciphertexts as
\{\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j}\}_{j\in[Q]}
which is used in answering
\mathcal{A}
’s secret key queries. When
\mathcal{A}
sends the challenge message tuple
(m_{0},m_{1})
,
\mathcal{B}_{7}
queries for a secret key with the pair of functions
(h[m_{1},m_{1},v],h[m_{1}])
to it’s challenger. Then,
\mathcal{B}_{7}
uses the output from it’s challenger to create the challenge ciphertext for
\mathcal{A}
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{B}_{7}
is an admissible adversary for the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since

h[m_{1},m_{1},v](\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},1)=h[m_{1}](\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0)

holds for all
j\in[Q]
. If
\mathcal{B}_{7}
receives ciphertexts for the messages
M_{j}^{(0)}
and a secret key
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{1},m_{1},v]}^{*}
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
, otherwise, if
\mathcal{B}_{7}
receives ciphertexts for the messages
M_{j}^{(1)}
and a secret key
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{1}]}^{*}
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
. Therefore, the winning probability of
\mathcal{B}_{7}
is essentially the same as
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}
. Hence, by the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(208)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
except the challenger samples

R_{j,i}=\mathsf{PRF}(\textsf{K}^{*},\tau_{j,i})~\text{ for all }i\in\{0,1,2,3\}

instead of sampling these values uniformly at random for all
j,i
, while answering the secret key queries. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{PRF}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\textsf{K}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of PRF. In other words, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{7}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(209)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{9}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
except the challenger computes

\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},\textsf{K}^{*},\bot,0))

instead of computing
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},(\bot,\bot,\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},1))
. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{9}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{PKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of PKFE. In other words, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{8}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{9}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(210)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{10}
:

This hybrid is identical to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{9}
except the challenger does not sample
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*}
and chooses
C_{j,\mathsf{ske}}
uniformly at random while answering to the
j
-th secret key query of
\mathcal{A}
for all
j
. More specifically, the challenger answers to the
j
-th key query for a function
f_{j}
as follows:

(a)

Sample
C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}=(\tau_{j,0}\|\tau_{j,1}\|\tau_{j,2}\|\tau_{j,3})
uniformly at random.

(b)

Generate
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}])
where
g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]
is a function described in
Figure

4
.

(c)

Set
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}
.

The challenger sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}}
to
\mathcal{A}
. The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{9}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{10}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{SKE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of SKE. In other words, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{9}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{10}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(211)

We observe that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{10}
is identical to original adaptive security experiment where the challenge bit set to
1
. Combining the advantage of
\mathcal{A}
in all the consecutive hybrids and applying the triangular inequality, we have
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{10}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

This completes the proof of
Theorem

B.21
if we prove
Lemma

B.22
.
∎

Lemma B.22.

If
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
is selectively single key function-private secure and public-slot SKFE
\mathsf{SKFE}
is adaptively single-key single-ciphertext secure then for any
\lambda\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(212)

Proof of
Lemma

B.22
.

We prove this lemma using a sequence of hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,1},\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2},\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3},\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,4}
for
q\in[Q]
where
Q
denotes the total number of secret key queried made by the adversary
\mathcal{A}
. Let us denote
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,Q+1,1}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,1}:

This is exactly the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
except the challenger sets
v
to be the output of
\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},m_{0};R_{q,2})
. More precisely, the hybrid works as follows:

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, sets
\mathsf{MPK}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK}
and
\mathsf{MSK}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK}
, and sends
\mathsf{MPK}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

The challenger generates a master secret key
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

The challenger generates a secret key
\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

4.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MPK},(\bot,\bot,\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},1))
.

5.

The challenger sets
u_{q}
as follows:

(a)

Sample
\tau_{q}=(\tau_{q,0}\|\tau_{q,1}\|\tau_{q,2}\|\tau_{q,3})
and
R_{q,i}
uniformly at random for all
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

(b)

Generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};R_{q,0})
.

(c)

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{q}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},f_{q};R_{q,1})
.

(d)

Compute
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{q}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},R_{q,2},0);R_{q,3})
.

(e)

Set
u_{q}=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{q}},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{q})
.

6.

\mathcal{A}
can make arbitrarily many key queries at any point of the experiment. When it makes the
j
-th key query for a function
f_{j}
, the challenger works as follows:

If
j\neq q:

(a)

Sample
\tau_{j}=(\tau_{j,0}\|\tau_{j,1}\|\tau_{j,2}\|\tau_{j,3})
and
R_{j,i}
uniformly at random for all
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

(b)

Generate
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};R_{j,0})
.

(c)

Compute
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},f_{j};R_{j,1})
.

(d)

Compute

\begin{array}[]{l l}\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j}\leftarrow&\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},1);R_{j,3})~~\text{ if }~j<q\\
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j}\leftarrow&\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0);R_{j,3})~~\text{ if }~j>q\\
\end{array}

(e)

Compute
C_{j,\mathsf{ske}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},u_{j})
where
u_{j}=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j})
.

(f)

Generate
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}])
where
g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]
is a function described in
Figure

4
.

(g)

Set
\mathsf{sk}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{j},C_{j,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{j}]}
.

If
j=q:

(a)

Set
C_{q,\mathsf{ske}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ske}.\mathsf{SK}^{*},u_{q}).

(b)

Generate
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{g[f_{q},C_{q,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{q}]}\leftarrow\mathsf{PKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{MSK},g[f_{q},C_{q,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{q}])
where
g[f_{q},C_{q,\mathsf{ske}},\tau_{q}]
is a function described in
Figure

4
.

The challenger sends
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{j}}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

7.

\mathcal{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})
to the challenger. It must satisfy
f(m_{0},y)=f(m_{1},y)
for any public input
y
and for all key queries
f
that are made before or after sending
(m_{0},m_{1})
.

8.

The challenger computes the ciphertext as follows:

(a)

Set
v\coloneqq\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},m_{0};R_{q,2})

(b)

Generate
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0},m_{1},v]}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},h[m_{0},m_{1},v])
where
h[m_{0},m_{1},v]
is a function described in
Figure

6
.

(c)

Set
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\coloneqq(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0},m_{1},v]}^{*},\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*})
where
\mathsf{pkfe}.\mathsf{ct}^{*}
is computed in Step 3.

The challenger sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

9.

\mathcal{A}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
which is the final output of the experiment.

The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,1,1}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
. Let us consider an adversary
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
against the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
.

At first,
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
prepares a list of
Q
challenge message pairs
(M_{1},\ldots,M_{Q})
where

\begin{array}[]{l l l}M_{j}=&(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},1)&\text{ if }1\leq j<q\\
M_{j}=&(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{j},R_{j,2},0)&\text{ if }q\leq j\leq Q.\\
\end{array}

for
j\in[Q]
. More precisely,
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
sends
(M_{j},M_{j})
for all
j\in[Q]
and receives the ciphertexts as
\{\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{j}\}_{j\in[Q]}
which is used in answering
\mathcal{A}
’s secret key queries. When
\mathcal{A}
sends the challenge message tuple
(m_{0},m_{1})
,
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
queries for a secret key with the pair of functions
(h[m_{0},m_{1},v],h[m_{0},m_{1},v^{\prime}])
to it’s challenger where
v
is a random string of appropriate length and
v^{\prime}=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},m_{0};R_{q,2})
. Then,
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
uses the output from it’s challenger to create the challenge ciphertext for
\mathcal{A}
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
is an admissible adversary for the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since
h[m_{0},m_{1},v](M_{j})=h[m_{0},m_{1},v^{\prime}](M_{j})
holds for all
j\in[Q]
. This is because
h[m_{0},m_{1},v](*,*,k)=h[m_{0},m_{1},v^{\prime}](*,*,k)
if
k\neq 2
. If
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
receives a secret key
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0},m_{1},v]}^{*}
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}
, otherwise, if
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
receives a secret key
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{h[m_{0},m_{1},v^{\prime}]}^{*}
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,1,1}
. Therefore, the winning probability of
\mathcal{B}_{4,1}
is essentially the same as
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,1,1}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}
. Hence, by the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,1,1}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(213)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2}:

This is exactly the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,1}
except the challenger changes the mode from
\alpha=0
to
\alpha=2
while decrypting the challenge ciphertext using the
q
-th secret key. More precisely, the challenger computes
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{q}
as follows:

\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{q}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},(0,0,2);R_{q,3}).

The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,1}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
. This can be shown similarly as we discussed in the previous hybrid since

h[m_{0},m_{1},v](\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},R_{q,2},0)=h[m_{0},m_{1},v](0,0,2)

holds where
v=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},m_{0};R_{q,2})
. Hence, by the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,1}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(214)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3}:

This is exactly the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2}
except the challenger changes
v
to be the encryption of
m_{1}
, that is, it sets
v
as follows:

v\coloneqq\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},m_{1};R_{q,2})

The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{SKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of SKFE. Let us consider an adversary
\mathcal{B}_{4,3}
against the security of adaptively single-key single-ciphertext secure public-slot SKFE. Note that,
\mathcal{B}_{4,3}
queries only a single secret key
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{q}}
corresponding to the function
f_{q}
and a single ciphertext
v
corresponding to the challenge message pair
(m_{0},m_{1})
. In particular,
\mathcal{B}_{4,3}
can adaptively query the secret key
\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{q}}
at any point whenever
\mathcal{A}
asks for a secret key for
f_{q}
and sets
u_{q}=(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{sk}_{f_{q}},\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{q})
. We observe that
\mathcal{B}_{4,3}
is an admissible adversary since
\mathcal{A}
is only allowed to query for a secret key for
f_{q}
and challenge message pair
(m_{0},m_{1})
such that
f_{q}(m_{0},y)=f_{q}(m_{1},y)
holds for is an arbitrary input
y
to the public slot of
f
. If
\mathcal{B}_{4,3}
receives a ciphertext
v=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},m_{0})
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2}
, otherwise, if
\mathcal{B}_{4,3}
receives a ciphertext
v=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},m_{1})
then it simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3}
. Therefore, the winning probability of
\mathcal{B}_{4,3}
is essentially the same as
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}
. Hence, by the security of SKFE, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,2}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(215)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,4}:

This is exactly the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3}
except the challenger changes the mode from
\alpha=2
to
\alpha=1
while decrypting the challenge ciphertext using the
q
-th secret key. More precisely, the challenger computes
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{q}
as follows:

\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{q}\leftarrow\mathsf{1KeySKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*},(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{ct}_{q},R_{q,2};1);R_{q,3}).

The indistinguishability between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,4}
follows from the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
since the view of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
can be simulated without the knowledge of
\mathsf{1keyskfe}.\mathsf{MSK}^{*}
and using the challenger of the security experiment of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
. This can be shown similarly as we discussed in the previous hybrid since

h[m_{0},m_{1},v](0,0,2)=h[m_{0},m_{1},v](\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},R_{q,2},1)

holds where
v=\mathsf{SKFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{skfe}.\mathsf{MSK}_{q},m_{1};R_{q,2})
. Hence, by the security of
\mathsf{1KeySKFE}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,3}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4,q,4}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(216)

Combining the advantage of
\mathcal{A}
in all the consecutive hybrids and applying the triangular inequality, we have
\left|\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}=1]-\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}=1]\right|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

B.22
.
∎

Appendix C
Secret and Public Key Encryption with Certified Everlasting Deletion

In
Section

C.1
, we define SKE and PKE with certified everlasting deletion.
In
Section

C.2
and
Section

C.3
,
we construct a certified everlasting secure SKE scheme with and without QROM, respectively.
In
Section

C.4
and
Section

C.5
, we construct a certified everlasting secure PKE scheme with and without QROM, respectively.

C.1
Definition

Definition C.1 (SKE with Certified Everlasting Deletion (Syntax)).

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and let
p
,
q
,
r
and
s
be some polynomials.
An SKE with certified everlasting deletion scheme is a tuple of algorithms
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{n}
, ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes p(\lambda)}
,
secret key space
\mathcal{SK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{q(\lambda)}
,
verification key space
\mathcal{VK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{r(\lambda)}
,
and deletion certificate space
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes s(\lambda)}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\mathsf{sk}
:

The key generation algorithm takes the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input and outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)\rightarrow(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})
:

The encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}
and a plaintext
m\in\mathcal{M}
as input, and outputs a verification key
\mathsf{vk}\in\mathcal{VK}
and a ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow m^{\prime}~or~\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}
and
\mathpzc{ct}
as input, and outputs a plaintext
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
or
\bot
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathsf{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm takes
\mathpzc{ct}
as input, and outputs a certification
\mathsf{cert}\in\mathcal{D}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top~\mbox{\bf or}~\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes
\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathsf{cert}
as input, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Remark
C.2.

Although we consider quantum certificates in
Section

C.3
, we consider classical certificates by default. In the quantum certificate case, we need to use
\mathpzc{cert}
and
\mathpzc{Vrfy}
in the syntax.

We require that an SKE with certified everlasting deletion scheme satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition C.3 (Correctness for SKE with Certified Everlasting Deletion).

There are three types of correctness, namely,
decryption correctness,
verification correctness, and special correctness.

Decryption Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[m^{\prime}\neq m\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Minimum requirements for correctness are decryption correctness and verification correctness.
However, we also require special correctness and verification correctness with QOTP in this work
because we need special correctness for the construction of the garbling scheme in
Section

E.2
,
and verification correctness with QOTP for the construction of FE in
Section

4.3
.

Definition C.4 (Special Correctness).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{sk}_{2},\mathpzc{ct})\neq\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{sk}_{2},\mathsf{sk}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk}_{1},m)\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Definition C.5 (Verification Correctness with QOTP).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
and a PPT algorithm
\mathsf{Recover}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert}^{*})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}\\
\widetilde{\mathsf{cert}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(Z^{b}X^{a}\mathpzc{ct}X^{a}Z^{b})\\
\mathsf{cert}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Recover}(a,b,\widetilde{\mathsf{cert}})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

As security, we consider two definitions,

Definition

C.6

and

Definition

C.7
given below.
The former is just the standard IND-CPA security
and the latter is the certified everlasting security
that we newly define in this paper.
Roughly, the everlasting security guarantees that any QPT adversary cannot obtain plaintext information even if it becomes computationally
unbounded and obtains the secret key after it issues a valid certificate.

Definition C.6 (IND-CPA Security for SKE with Certified Everlasting Deletion).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\allowbreak\mathsf{Vrfy})
be an SKE with certified everlasting deletion scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

1.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends an encryption query
m
to the challenger.
The challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)
, and returns
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

\mathpzc{A}
can repeat this process polynomially many times.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m_{b})
, and sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
sends an encryption query
m
to the challenger.
The challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m)
, and returns
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

\mathpzc{A}
can repeat this process polynomially many times.

6.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. This is the output of the experiment.

We say that
\Sigma
is IND-CPA secure if, for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[ \expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ind}{cpa}(\secp, 0)=1] - \Pr[ \expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ind}{cpa}(\secp, 1)=1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(230)

Definition C.7 (Certified Everlasting IND-CPA Security for SKE).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a certified everlasting SKE scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
and an unbounded adversary
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.

1.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
sends an encryption query
m_{i}
to the challenger.
The challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathpzc{ct}_{i})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m_{i})
, and returns
(\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathpzc{ct}_{i})
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
can repeat this process polynomially many times.

3.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

4.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m_{b})
, and sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
sends an encryption query
m_{i}
to the challenger.
The challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathpzc{ct}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},m_{i})
, and returns
(\mathsf{vk}_{i},\mathpzc{ct}_{i})
to
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
.

\mathpzc{A}_{1}
can repeat this process polynomially many times.

6.

At some point,
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger and sends the internal state to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.

7.

The challenger computes
\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
.
If the output is
\bot
, the challenger outputs
\bot
, and sends
\bot
to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.
Otherwise, the challenger outputs
\top
, and sends
\mathsf{sk}
to
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
.

8.

\mathpzc{A}_{2}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
.

9.

If the challenger outputs
\top
, then the output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the output of the experiment is
\bot
.

We say that
\Sigma
is certified everlasting IND-CPA secure if, for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}_{1}
and any unbounded
\mathpzc{A}_{2}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{cert}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ever}\mbox{-}\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[ \expd{\Sigma,\qA}{cert}{ever}{ind}{cpa}(\secp, 0)=1] - \Pr[ \expd{\Sigma,\qA}{cert}{ever}{ind}{cpa}(\secp, 1)=1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(231)

Definition C.8 (PKE with Certified Everlasting Deletion (Syntax)).

Let
\lambda
be a security parameter and let
p
,
q
,
r
,
s
and
t
be polynomials.
A PKE with certified everlasting deletion scheme is a tuple of algorithms
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})

with plaintext space
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{n}
,
ciphertext space
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes p(\lambda)}
,
public key space
\mathcal{PK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{q(\lambda)}
, secret key space
\mathcal{SK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{r(\lambda)}
,
verification key space
\mathcal{VK}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{s(\lambda)}

and deletion certificate space
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\mathcal{Q}^{\otimes t(\lambda)}
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
:

The key generation algorithm takes the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input and outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}\in\mathcal{PK}
and a secret key
\mathsf{sk}\in\mathcal{SK}
.

\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)\rightarrow(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})
:

The encryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{pk}
and a plaintext
m\in\mathcal{M}
as input,
and outputs a verification key
\mathsf{vk}\in\mathcal{VK}
and a ciphertext
\mathpzc{ct}\in\mathcal{C}
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow m^{\prime}~or~\bot
:

The decryption algorithm takes
\mathsf{sk}
and
\mathpzc{ct}
as input, and outputs a plaintext
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
or
\bot
.

\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\rightarrow\mathsf{cert}
:

The deletion algorithm takes
\mathpzc{ct}
as input and outputs a certification
\mathsf{cert}\in\mathcal{D}
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})\rightarrow\top
or
\bot
:

The verification algorithm takes
\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathsf{cert}
as input, and outputs
\top
or
\bot
.

Remark
C.9.

Although we consider quantum certificates in
Section

C.5
, we consider classical certificates by default. In the quantum certificate case, we need to use
\mathpzc{cert}
and
\mathpzc{Vrfy}
in the syntax.

We require that a PKE with certified everlasting deletion scheme satisfies correctness defined below.

Definition C.10 (Correctness for PKE with Certified Everlasting Deletion).

There are two types of correctness, namely, decryption correctness and verification correctness.

Decryption Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[m^{\prime}\neq m\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)\\
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sk},\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Verification Correctness:

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)\\
\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(\mathpzc{ct})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

Minimum requirements for correctness are decryption correctness and verification correctness.
However, we also require verification correctness with QOTP in this work because we need it for the construction of FE in
Section

4.3
.

Definition C.11 (Verification Correctness with QOTP).

There exists a negligible function
{\mathsf{negl}}
and a PPT algorithm
\mathsf{Recover}
such that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert}^{*})=\bot\ \middle|\begin{array}[]{ll}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m)\\
a,b\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{p(\lambda)}\\
\widetilde{\mathsf{cert}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{Del}(Z^{b}X^{a}\mathpzc{ct}X^{a}Z^{b})\\
\mathsf{cert}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Recover}(a,b,\widetilde{\mathsf{cert}})\end{array}\right]\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

As security, we consider two definitions,

Definition

C.12

and

Definition

C.13
given below.
The former is just the standard IND-CPA security
and the latter is the certified everlasting security
that we newly define in this paper.
Roughly, the everlasting security guarantees that any QPT adversary cannot obtain plaintext information even if it becomes computationally unbounded and obtains the secret key after it issues a valid certificate.

Definition C.12 (IND-CPA Security for PKE with Certified Everlasting Deletion).

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Del},\allowbreak\mathsf{Vrfy})
be a PKE with certified everlasting deletion scheme.
We consider the following security experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda,b)
against a QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
, and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

\mathpzc{A}
sends
(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
to the challenger.

The challenger computes
(\mathsf{vk},\mathpzc{ct})\leftarrow\mathpzc{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m_{b})
, and sends
\mathpzc{ct}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. This is the output of the experiment.

We say that the
\Sigma
is IND-CPA secure if, for any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\Sigma,\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ind}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ind}{cpa}(\secp,0)=1] - \Pr[\expb{\Sigma,\qA}{ind}{cpa}(\secp,1)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(243)

Let
\Sigma=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathpzc{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Del},\mathsf{Vrfy})

against a QPT adversary

and an unbounded adversary

to

, and sends
